Polycontrolled PROPs for Qudit Circuits: A Uniform Complete Equational Theory For Arbitrary Finite Dimension
Colin Blake

Abstract
. We present a finite schematic axiomatisation of quantum circuits over d d -level systems (qudits), uniform in every finite dimension d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 . For each d d we define a PROP CQC d \textbf{{CQC}}_{d} equipped with a family of control functors, treating control as a primitive categorical constructor. Using a translation between qudit circuits and the LOPP calculus for linear optics based on d d -ary Gray codes, we obtain for each d d a finite set of local axiom schemata QC d \mathrm{QC}_{d} that is sound and complete for unitary d d -level circuits: two circuits denote the same unitary if and only if they are inter-derivable using axioms involving at most three wires. The generators are compatible with standard universal qudit gate families, yielding a sound equational basis for circuit rewriting and optimisation-by-rewriting. Conceptually, this extends the qubit circuit completeness results of Clément et al. to arbitrary finite dimension, and instantiates the control-as-constructor approach of Delorme and Perdrix in this setting, while keeping the axiom shapes uniform in d d .



1.
Introduction

Quantum circuits provide a standard low-level syntax for finite-dimensional
quantum computation: wires carry
d
-level quantum data, boxes represent unitary
gates, and a circuit denotes a unitary operator on the tensor power

(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}

(
15
;
24
)
.
Accordingly, they serve as the basic representation both for algorithm design
and for hardware-aware compilation and verification; in this setting,
optimisation is typically justified
via
equational circuit rewriting.
Moreover, correctness arguments in verification and compilation are routinely
phrased as local diagrammatic rewrites, closed under both sequential and
parallel composition.
Consequently, from a logical perspective, this raises a foundational
completeness question: can equality of unitary circuits be axiomatised by a

finite
, syntax-directed equational theory in the circuit PROP?

In the PROP formalism (a strict symmetric monoidal category whose objects are
wire counts) such a theory is given by a finite set of local rewrite

schemata
between circuit diagrams, closed under both sequential and
parallel composition.
For qubits (
d=2
), complete circuit-level axiomatisations are already known

(
8
;
9
;
6
)
.
A key lesson from this line of work is the central role of
control
.
If control is not treated as primitive, then completeness forces axiom
schemata that quantify over arbitrarily many control wires. By contrast,
treating control functorially in a controlled PROP yields finite, bounded-arity
axiom sets
(
14
;
17
)
.

Meanwhile, graphical calculi such as ZX/ZH/ZW admit complete equational
theories for broad classes of qubit processes

(
11
;
28
;
19
;
1
;
4
;
5
)
.
However, these languages describe general linear maps rather than
circuit-level unitaries; moreover, extracting circuits can be non-local—and,
indeed, hard in general
(
12
)
. This motivates circuit-specific
rewrite systems.

For
d>2
, many platforms support native
d
-level systems (qudits);
consequently, qudit-native design and compilation can reduce resource counts or
exploit non-binary structure in gate libraries and higher-dimensional circuit
design

(
20
;
31
;
25
;
27
)
.
From an equational perspective, several diagrammatic and circuit formalisms for qudits
have been proposed.
Qudit and “qufinite” variants of the ZX- and ZH-calculi provide universal
graphical languages parameterised by the dimension; moreover, they admit
completeness results in some settings
(
34
;
30
)
.
More recently, qufinite ZXW-calculi, together with finite-dimensional ZX and
ZW calculi, have yielded complete equational theories for the whole of
finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, uniformly in
d

(
33
;
26
;
13
)
.
These results are foundationally powerful; nevertheless, they operate at the
level of
arbitrary
linear maps and therefore do not directly yield
bounded-arity PROP presentations of a circuit language for
unitary
gates with a native
control constructor.
At the circuit level, by contrast, completeness results are largely
fragmentary. There are complete rewrite systems for specific subtheories, notably for qutrit
stabiliser quantum mechanics in ZX and, more recently, for multi-qutrit Clifford
circuits
(
20
)
.
Beyond ZX-style diagrammatics, further work has developed graphical calculi
for multi-qudit computations based on generalised Clifford algebras and related
algebraic structures
(
21
;
22
)
.
However, these approaches either target broad classes of linear maps (rather
than a circuit syntax for unitary gates), focus on particular fragments, or
treat only fixed small dimensions.
Consequently, they do not provide a finite, uniform-in-
d
PROP presentation of
the
circuit
category whose objects are wire arities and whose morphisms
are unitary
d
-level circuits over a fixed universal gate signature, with axioms
given by local schemata of bounded wire arity.

There is no dimension-uniform finitary presentation of unitary qudit
circuits satisfying the following combination of requirements. Namely,
for each

d\geq 2
, one seeks a sound and complete
finite
set of circuit-specific
axiom schemata for the PROP of unitary
d
-level circuits over a fixed universal
gate signature, with axioms given by local schemata of bounded wire arity and
schematic form independent of
d
.
This contrasts with the qubit case

(
8
;
9
;
6
;
14
)

and, as a result, leaves qudit equational reasoning without a uniform
completeness theory.
Moreover, beyond foundational interest, the absence of such a fixed rewrite
system makes it harder to justify rewriting-based optimisation and verification
of native qudit circuits without resorting to ad hoc matrix calculations.

At a high level, we treat control as a primitive operation in a
polycontrolled PROP.
Control is fundamental in circuit design and compilation:
given a subcircuit
f:n\to n
, one forms a controlled version by adjoining a
new wire and applying
f
conditionally; importantly, this construction must be
coherent with sequential composition and wire permutations.
Rather than treating each controlled-
U
gate as an ad hoc derived primitive,
we build control directly into the categorical structure itself

(
14
;
17
)
.
Concretely, we equip the circuit language with a family of control functors

(\mathtt{C}_{k})_{k=0}^{d-1}
. Each
\mathtt{C}_{k}
sends an endomorphism
f:n\to n
to an
endomorphism
\mathtt{C}_{k}(f):1+n\to 1+n
, which applies
f
exactly when the added
control qudit is in computational basis state
\left|k\right\rangle
(and otherwise acts as
the identity on the targets). Multi-controlled operations are obtained
systematically by iterating these functors.
Treating control as a first-class constructor is what makes bounded-arity
axioms possible: local schemata can refer to
\mathtt{C}_{k}(-)
itself, rather than
quantifying over arbitrarily many explicit control wires.

Main result.

For each finite
d\geq 2
, we define a polycontrolled circuit PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.
Moreover, we introduce a finite equational theory
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
generated by
local axiom schemata whose schematic form is uniform in
d
and whose instance
involve at most three wires.
Theorem
4.19
states that
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
is sound and
complete for the standard unitary semantics

\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}\to\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}
: for any
n
-qudit circuits
C_{1},C_{2}:n\to n
,

\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}

Here, “uniformity” has three aspects: (i) bounded wire arity; (ii) a fixed,
finite list of axiom
schema families
whose diagrammatic shape does not
depend on
d
; and (iii) only indices (in
[d]
) vary with
d
.

Our completeness proof proceeds by reduction to the linear-optical calculus

LOPP
. In particular, following Clément et al.
(
8
)
, we
transfer completeness from
LOPP

(
7
;
18
)
.
Using a reflected
d
-ary Gray code, we encode an
n
-qudit circuit as a

d^{n}
-mode single-photon linear-optical circuit. This encoding preserves the
intended unitary semantics, up to conjugation by a fixed Gray-code basis
permutation.
A key technical point is that
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
and
LOPP
interpret
\otimes

differently (tensor product versus direct sum under single-photon semantics).
Consequently, the translation cannot be strictly monoidal; instead, we fix a
deterministic serialisation of
\otimes
and prove an encoding/decoding
retraction.
We then transport completeness of
LOPP
back to
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
via decoding
and a “mimicking” argument.
When
d=2
, the reflected
d
-ary Gray code specialises to the binary
reflected Gray code; correspondingly, our reduction to
LOPP
recovers the
qubit-level completeness-transfer pattern of
(
8
)
.

For each finite
d\geq 2
we obtain:

(1)

A polycontrolled circuit PROP.

We define the endomorphism PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
of
d
-level
unitary circuits, equipped with control functors
(\mathtt{C}_{k})_{k=0}^{d-1}
whose
iteration yields multi-controlled operations.

(2)

A finite bounded-arity axiom system.

We present a finite set of local axiom schemata
\mathrm{QC}_{d}

(each instance uses at most three wires) of schematic form uniform in
d
,
and show that it is sound and complete for unitary
d
-level circuit equality.
This yields a fixed, syntax-directed rewrite system suitable as a foundation
for rewriting-based optimisation and verification of native qudit circuits.

(3)

A completeness transfer via Gray-code embedding.

Using a
d
-ary Gray code, we encode
n
-qudit circuits as
d^{n}
-mode

LOPP
circuits and decode
LOPP
derivations back into
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
,
yielding a modular completeness proof via
(
7
;
18
)
.

(4)

Expressing standard qudit gate families.

Our generators and axioms express adjacent two-level gates and
basis-controlled phases. Consequently, one can assemble common universal qudit
gate families (as recalled in §
2.4
). This keeps the presentation
circuit-level and dimension-uniform, whilst remaining compatible with standard
synthesis practice.

Throughout, we work with
exact
unitary equality: global phases are

not
quotiented away and therefore appear as scalar (
0\to 0
) morphisms.
Our goal is a circuit-specific presentation of the PROP of unitary circuits over
a fixed signature; unlike the uniform-in-
d
completeness results for
finite-dimensional ZX-/ZW-/ZXW-calculi

(
33
;
26
;
13
)
, we do not aim to
axiomatise arbitrary linear maps.

The paper is organised as follows.
Section
2
defines
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
and its unitary semantics;
Section
3
presents the axiom schemata generating

\mathrm{QC}_{d}
; and Section
4
give the Gray-code
translation to
LOPP
and the completeness transfer culminating in
Theorem
4.19
.

2.
Qudit circuits as a polycontrolled PROP

In this section we set up the circuit language used throughout this work.
Fix a dimension
d\geq 2
.
Our goal is to work in a
circuit-level
setting: objects are wire counts,
morphisms are unitary
d
-level circuits, and circuit equality will ultimately
be captured by a finite, local axiom system.
The key design choice is that
control is primitive
.
Rather than treating each controlled gate as a separate derived gadget, we build
control into the categorical structure: for every computational basis value

k\in[d]
we have a constructor
\mathtt{C}_{k}(-)
that turns a circuit
f:n\to n
into
its
k
-controlled version
\mathtt{C}_{k}(f):1+n\to 1+n
.
Multi-controlled operations are then obtained simply by
iterating
\mathtt{C}_{k}
.

2.1.
PROPs and (poly)controlled PROPs

We work in a strict symmetric monoidal setting, so wire counts form the objects,
parallel composition is strictly associative, and diagrams are taken modulo the
strict coherence laws.

Definition 2.1 (
(
23
)
).

A
PROP

P
is a strict symmetric monoidal category whose objects
are the natural numbers, generated by a distinguished object
1
with tensor

m\otimes n=m+n

and unit object
0
.

We write
\textbf{{P}}(m,n)
for the hom-set
m\to n
.
Composition is written
\circ
, tensor (parallel composition) as
\otimes
,
and
\sigma_{m,n}:m+n\to n+m
denotes the symmetry (wire swap).

Since our circuits are unitary, we only consider endomorphisms.
For a PROP
P
, write
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{endo}}
for its wide
endomorphism subcategory:
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{endo}}(n,n)=\textbf{{P}}(n,n)
and

\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{endo}}(m,n)=\varnothing
for
m\neq n
.

A permutation is any morphism
\pi:n\to n
obtained from identities and
symmetries
\sigma_{m,n}
using only
\circ
and
\otimes
. Such morphisms
are canonically invertible; we write
\pi^{-1}
for the inverse permutation.

Informally, “adding a control” is an operation that, given an endomorphism

f:n\to n
, produces an endomorphism on one additional wire that applies
f

conditionally. Categorically, we package this as an endofunctor on

\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{endo}}
that adjoins a distinguished wire and behaves well
with respect to the PROP structure.

Definition 2.2 (
(
14
)
).

Let
P
be a PROP and
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{endo}}
its endomorphism PROP.
A
control functor
is a functor

\mathtt{C}:\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{endo}}\to\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{endo}}
such that:

•

on objects,
\mathtt{C}(n)=1+n
(it adjoins one distinguished
control wire
);

•

on morphisms, for every
f:n\to n
, every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and every
permutation
\pi:n\to n
, the morphism
\mathtt{C}(f):1+n\to 1+n
satisfies:

(1)

\displaystyle\mathtt{C}(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{m})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{C}(f)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{m},

(2)

\displaystyle\mathtt{C}(\mathtt{C}(f))\circ(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n})

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n})\circ\mathtt{C}(\mathtt{C}(f)),

(3)

\displaystyle\mathtt{C}(\pi^{-1}\circ f\circ\pi)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\pi^{-1})\circ\mathtt{C}(f)\circ(\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\pi).

The three coherence laws can be read as follows.
Equation (
1
) says that unused wires remain untouched.
Equation (
2
) says that nesting control twice is
independent of the order of the two control wires, up to swapping them.
Equation (
3
) says that controlling is invariant under
permuting the target wires: if you relabel the target register, controlling
commutes with that relabelling.

Definition 2.3 (
(
14
)
).

A
polycontrolled PROP
is a PROP
P
equipped with a family of
control functors
(\mathtt{C}_{k})_{k\in K}
on
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{endo}}
, indexed by
a set
K
.
When
K=[d]=\{0,\dots,d-1\}
we speak of a
d
-ary
polycontrolled PROP.

We use the usual string-diagram notation for PROPs: diagrams are read
left-to-right; sequential composition
D\circ C
is plugging; tensor

C\otimes D
is juxtaposition; and symmetries
\sigma_{m,n}
are wire swaps.
In a polycontrolled PROP,
\mathtt{C}_{k}(f)
is depicted by adjoining a distinguished
control wire (drawn above the targets) labelled by
k
.

\mathrm{id}_{0}

\mathrm{id}_{1}

\sigma_{1,1}

D\circ C

C\otimes D

\mathtt{C}_{k}(C)

Fix
d\geq 2
, and let
P
be a
d
-ary polycontrolled PROP with control
functors
(\mathtt{C}_{k})_{k\in[d]}
.
For a word
u=u_{1}\cdots u_{m}\in[d]^{*}
and an endomorphism
f:n\to n
, we write

\mathtt{C}_{u}(f):|u|+n\to|u|+n
for the iterated control of
f
by
u
, defined
by
\mathtt{C}_{\epsilon}(f):=f
and
\mathtt{C}_{ku}(f):=\mathtt{C}_{k}(\mathtt{C}_{u}(f))
.
Equivalently,
\mathtt{C}_{u}(f)=\mathtt{C}_{u_{1}}(\cdots(\mathtt{C}_{u_{m}}(f))\cdots)
.

Definition 2.4.

Let
d\geq 2
and let
P
be a
d
-ary polycontrolled PROP equipped with

(\mathtt{C}_{k})_{k=0}^{d-1}
.

(1)

The family
(\mathtt{C}_{k})
is
compatible
if for all
n\geq 0
, all

f:n\to n
, and all
a,b\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
, nesting
a
-control and

b
-control commutes up to swapping the resulting control wires:

(
Compat
)

=

\mathtt{C}_{ab}(f)\circ(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n})

=

(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n})\circ\mathtt{C}_{ba}(f)

(2)

The family
(\mathtt{C}_{k})
is
commutative
if for all
n\geq 0
, all

f,g:n\to n
, and all distinct
a,b\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
, operations
controlled on distinct basis values commute:

(
Comm
)

=

\mathtt{C}_{a}(f)\circ\mathtt{C}_{b}(g)

=

\mathtt{C}_{b}(g)\circ\mathtt{C}_{a}(f)

(3)

The family
(\mathtt{C}_{k})
is
exhaustive
if for all
n\geq 0
and all

f:n\to n
, controlling
f
on every basis value collapses to an
unconditional application of
f
on the targets (leaving the control wire
intact):

(
Exh
)

=

\mathtt{C}_{0}(f)\circ\mathtt{C}_{1}(f)\circ\cdots\circ\mathtt{C}_{d-1}(f)

=

\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes f

In the circuit PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
defined below, we
do not
impose any equations
relating
\mathtt{C}_{a}
and
\mathtt{C}_{b}
at the level of structural equality.
Instead, the finite equational theory
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
will be used to
derive

the global control-algebra laws in Section
3.3
.

2.2.
Raw circuits and the PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}

We now fix a dimension
d\geq 2
and define a circuit syntax with explicit
control constructors.
We deliberately separate two layers:

•

raw circuits
, which are syntax trees built from generators using
term-formers
\circ
,
\otimes
, and
\mathtt{C}_{k}(-)
; and

•

circuits in the PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
, obtained by quotienting raw syntax
by the PROP coherence laws and the coherence laws that make each

\mathtt{C}_{k}
into a control functor.

Keeping the raw syntax is useful later, because our Gray-code
encoding/decoding maps are defined by structural induction on syntax.

Let
\Sigma_{QC_{d}}
consist of the following endomorphism generators:

\scalebox{0.8}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}:0\to 0,\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}:1\to 1,\qquad\scalebox{0.5}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}:2\to 2,

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$}:1\to 1\quad(r\in[d-1]),\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}:0\to 0\quad(\theta\in\mathbb{R}).

In particular,
\Sigma_{QC_{d}}
is infinite because it contains a generator for
every real parameter
\theta
.

Definition 2.5.

Fix
d\geq 2
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
be the set of

raw
n
-wire circuits
, defined as the smallest family of sets

(\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
closed under:

(1)

Generators.
For every generator
g:n\to n
of
\Sigma_{QC_{d}}
,

g
is in
\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
.

(2)

Sequential composition.
If
C,D\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
then

D\circ C\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
.

(3)

Parallel composition.
If
C\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(m,m)
and

D\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
then
C\otimes D\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(m+n,m+n)
.

(4)

Control.
For each
k\in[d]
, if
C\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
then

\mathtt{C}_{k}(C)\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(1+n,1+n)
.

Thus
\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}
is the free term algebra generated by
\Sigma_{QC_{d}}
using

\circ
,
\otimes
, and the unary constructors
\mathtt{C}_{k}(-)
.
A raw term records the syntactic structure of a controlled circuit.

We will occasionally need explicit raw representatives for identities and
permutations (e.g. when stating coherence axioms).
For each
n\geq 0
let
\mathrm{id}_{n}\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
be the canonical raw identity:

\mathrm{id}_{0}:=\scalebox{0.8}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}
and

\mathrm{id}_{n+1}:=\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}
.

Permutation terms are raw circuits generated from identities and the swap

using only
\circ
and
\otimes
; they denote wire permutations
and are canonically invertible.
In particular, for each
m,n\geq 0
we fix a canonical
block-swap

permutation term
\sigma_{m,n}:m+n\to n+m
, defined recursively by

\sigma_{0,n}:=\mathrm{id}_{n}
,
\sigma_{m,0}:=\mathrm{id}_{m}
,
\sigma_{1,1}:=\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}
,

\sigma_{m,1}:=(\sigma_{m-1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{1})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{m-1}\otimes\sigma_{1,1})
,
and

\sigma_{m,n}:=(\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\sigma_{m,n-1})\circ(\sigma_{m,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n-1})
.

The raw syntax carries more parentheses and wiring bookkeeping than we want to
treat as meaningful. We therefore quotient by the least congruence that forces:
(i) the strict PROP laws; and (ii) the axioms saying that each
\mathtt{C}_{k}
really
is a control functor.

Definition 2.6.

Let
\equiv
be the least congruence on
\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}
—that is, an equivalence
relation on each
\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
, closed under all contexts built from
\circ
,

\otimes
, and each
\mathtt{C}_{k}
, that contains all well-typed instances of:

•

the strict PROP equations
(
23
)
(associativity/unit for
\circ

and
\otimes
, interchange, symmetry naturality and involutivity); and

•

for each
k\in[d]
, the functoriality and coherence equations for

\mathtt{C}_{k}
from Definition
2.2
:

\mathtt{C}_{k}(\mathrm{id}_{n})\equiv\mathrm{id}_{1+n}
,

\mathtt{C}_{k}(D\circ C)\equiv\mathtt{C}_{k}(D)\circ\mathtt{C}_{k}(C)
,
and the coherence schemata (
1
)–(
3
)
(with
\pi
ranging over permutation terms).

Thus
C\equiv C^{\prime}
means that
C
and
C^{\prime}
differ only by the PROP coherence
and by the coherence laws that make each
\mathtt{C}_{k}
a well-behaved control
constructor. No gate-specific equations are imposed here; those enter later via
the equational theory
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

Definition 2.7.

Define the
endomorphism PROP

\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
by

\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(n,n):=\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)\big/\equiv,\qquad\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(m,n):=\varnothing\ \text{if }m\neq n,

with
\circ
and
\otimes
induced by the term constructors on
representatives, and with symmetry
\sigma_{m,n}
induced by the corresponding
raw permutation terms.

Write
[{-}]_{\equiv}:\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)\to\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(n,n)
for the quotient map.
By construction, each
\mathtt{C}_{k}
respects
\equiv
and therefore descends to
an endofunctor on
(\textbf{{CQC}}_{d})_{\mathrm{endo}}
satisfying
Definition
2.2
.
We call
\left(\textbf{{CQC}}_{d},(\mathtt{C}_{k})_{k=0}^{d-1}\right)
the
polycontrolled PROP
of
d
-level quantum circuits
.

2.3.
Qudit semantics

We now fix the intended semantics: circuits denote unitaries on

(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
.
Fix
d\geq 2
and recall
[d]:=\{0,1,\dots,d-1\}
.
Let
\mathbb{C}^{d}
be the
d
-dimensional Hilbert space with
computational basis

\{\left|0\right\rangle,\dots,\left|d-1\right\rangle\}
.
For
n\geq 0
, set
\mathcal{H}_{n}:=(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}\cong\mathbb{C}^{d^{n}}
,
with basis
\{\left|x\right\rangle\mid x\in[d]^{n}\}
, and write
I_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}
for the
identity on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

Recall that
U:\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\mathcal{H}_{n}
is
unitary
if

U^{\dagger}U=UU^{\dagger}=I_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}
, equivalently
U^{-1}=U^{\dagger}
.
We write
U(N):=\{U\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}\mid U^{\dagger}U=UU^{\dagger}=I_{N}\}
.

Definition 2.8.

Let
\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}
be the
endomorphism PROP
defined by the hom-sets

\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(n,n):=U(d^{n}),\qquad\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(m,n):=\varnothing\ \text{if }m\neq n,

where we identify
\mathcal{H}_{n}\cong\mathbb{C}^{d^{n}}
via the computational basis.
Composition is ordinary composition of linear maps (matrix multiplication),
tensor on morphisms is the tensor product of operators, and the symmetry

\sigma_{m,n}:m+n\to n+m
is the swap unitary determined by

\sigma_{m,n}(\left|x\right\rangle\otimes\left|y\right\rangle):=\left|y\right\rangle\otimes\left|x\right\rangle\qquad(x\in[d]^{m},\ y\in[d]^{n}).

In particular,
\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(0,0)\cong U(1)=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid|z|=1\}
consists of
global phases.

Given any unitary
U\in\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(n,n)
and a basis value
k\in[d]
, define its

k
-
controlled extension

\mathsf{C}_{k}(U)\in\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(n+1,n+1)
by

(4)

\mathsf{C}_{k}(U):=\left|k\right\rangle\left\langle k\right|\otimes U+\sum_{\ell\in[d],\,\ell\neq k}\left|\ell\right\rangle\left\langle\ell\right|\otimes I_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}.

Semantically,
\mathsf{C}_{k}(U)
applies
U
to the target register iff the added
control qudit is in state
\left|k\right\rangle
, and otherwise acts as the identity.

Definition 2.9.

Define, by structural induction, a family of maps

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)\longrightarrow\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(n,n)\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N})

as follows.

•

Generators.
On the generators of
\Sigma_{QC_{d}}
set:

–

\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket:=1
,

\left\llbracket\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\right\rrbracket:=e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
,

\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket:=\sum_{k\in[d]}\left|k\right\rangle\left\langle k\right|
,

–

\left\llbracket\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket:=\sum_{i,j\in[d]}\left|j,i\right\rangle\left\langle i,j\right|
,

–

\begin{aligned} \left\llbracket\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$}\right\rrbracket&:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in[d]\backslash\{r,r+1\}\end{subarray}}\left|k\right\rangle\left\langle k\right|\\
&+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\left|r\right\rangle+\left|r+1\right\rangle\right)\left\langle r\right|\\
&+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\left|r\right\rangle-\left|r+1\right\rangle\right)\left\langle r+1\right|.\end{aligned}

•

Sequential and parallel composition.
For raw circuits
C,D
,
set

\left\llbracket D\circ C\right\rrbracket:=\left\llbracket D\right\rrbracket\circ\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket,\qquad\left\llbracket C\otimes D\right\rrbracket:=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket\otimes\left\llbracket D\right\rrbracket.

•

Control.
For
C\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
and
k\in[d]
, set

(5)

\left\llbracket\mathtt{C}_{k}(C)\right\rrbracket:=\mathsf{C}_{k}\bigl(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket\bigr)\in\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(1+n,1+n).

Proposition 2.10
.

The interpretation
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)\to\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(n,n)
respects the
structural congruence
\equiv
of Definition
2.6
.
Hence it factors uniquely through the quotient to a well-defined strict
symmetric monoidal functor

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}\longrightarrow\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d},

satisfying
\left\llbracket[C]_{\equiv}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket
for every raw circuit
C
.

Proof.

The strict PROP equations are respected because
\circ
is interpreted as
composition of linear maps,
\otimes
as tensor product, and the swap generator
as the swap unitary.
For control, the functoriality of
\mathsf{C}_{k}(-)
together with
(
4
) yields
\left\llbracket\mathtt{C}_{k}(\mathrm{id}_{n})\right\rrbracket=I_{\mathcal{H}_{n+1}}

and
\left\llbracket\mathtt{C}_{k}(D\circ C)\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket\mathtt{C}_{k}(D)\right\rrbracket\circ\left\llbracket\mathtt{C}_{k}(C)\right\rrbracket
.
The coherence axioms (
1
)–(
3
) hold
because
\mathsf{C}_{k}(-)
acts only on the additional control tensor factor and is
therefore insensitive to unused wires and to permutations of the target wires.
∎

2.4.
Universality

Fix
d\geq 2
.
We now record that our gate signature
\Sigma_{QC_{d}}
is universal for

d
-level unitary computation: every unitary
U\in U(d^{n})
is denoted by some
circuit in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(n,n)
.

We rely on two standard synthesis facts.
First, every
2\times 2
unitary admits an Euler decomposition in terms of a
Hadamard and computational-basis phases

(
24
;
2
)
:
for all
U\in U(2)
there exist
\theta,\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
such that

U=e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\,Z(\alpha)\,H\,Z(\beta)\,H\,Z(\gamma),

where
H\in U(2)
is the Hadamard and
Z(\phi)=\mathrm{diag}(1,e^{\mathrm{i}\phi})
.

Second,
U(d)
is generated by
adjacent two-level
unitaries

(
24
;
10
;
29
;
16
)
: every
V\in U(d)

is a product of unitaries, each supported on

\mathrm{span}\{\left|r\right\rangle,\left|r+1\right\rangle\}
for some
r\in[d-1]
.

For convenience, define

Z_{d}^{(j)}(\theta):=\left\llbracket\mathtt{C}_{j}(\scalebox{0.8}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}})\right\rrbracket,\qquad H_{d}^{(r,r+1)}:=\left\llbracket\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$}\right\rrbracket.

Lemma 2.11
.

Fix
r\in[d-1]
. Let
W\in U(d)
act as the identity on the orthogonal
complement of
\mathrm{span}\{\left|r\right\rangle,\left|r+1\right\rangle\}
.
Then there exists a
1
-qudit circuit
C_{W}\in\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(1,1)
such that

\left\llbracket C_{W}\right\rrbracket=W
.

Proof.

Restrict
W
to
\mathrm{span}\{\left|r\right\rangle,\left|r+1\right\rangle\}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
By the Euler decomposition, this restriction factors (up to a global phase) as a
product of
2\times 2
Hadamards and computational-basis phases.
Embedding back into
\mathbb{C}^{d}
, each factor becomes either an adjacent
Hadamard
H_{d}^{(r,r+1)}
, a level-phase
Z_{d}^{(j)}(\cdot)
, or a global phase.
Each is realised by a circuit in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
, and composing them yields
C_{W}
.
∎

Define the two-qudit unitary

CZ_{d}:=I_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}-2\left|0,0\right\rangle\left\langle 0,0\right|\in U(d^{2}).

Equivalently,
CZ_{d}
sends
\left|0,0\right\rangle\mapsto-\left|0,0\right\rangle
and fixes all other
computational basis states. In
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
,

\left\llbracket\mathtt{C}_{0}(\mathtt{C}_{0}(\scalebox{0.8}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/piphase.pdf}}}}}}}$}}))\right\rrbracket=CZ_{d}.

We use the standard universality fact that for all
n\geq 0
, arbitrary
single-qudit unitaries together with
CZ_{d}
generate
U(d^{n})

(
3
)
.

Proposition 2.12
.

For every
n\geq 0
, the interpretation map

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(n,n)\to\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(n,n)=U(d^{n})

is surjective.

Proof.

Let
U\in U(d^{n})
.

If
n=0
, then
U\in U(1)
is a scalar phase
e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
, denoted
by

.

Assume
n\geq 1
.
By adjacent two-level generation of
U(d)
, every single-qudit unitary can be
written as a product of unitaries supported on
\mathrm{span}\{\left|r\right\rangle,\left|r+1\right\rangle\}
.
By Lemma
2.11
, each such factor is realised in

\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(1,1)
, hence arbitrary single-qudit unitaries are realised.

Together with
CZ_{d}
, these single-qudit unitaries generate
U(d^{n})
.
The gate
CZ_{d}
is realised in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
, and the PROP structure (tensoring with
identities and permutations) allows placing these realised 1- and 2-qudit
circuits on any desired wire(s). Composing the resulting circuits yields some

C\in\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(n,n)
with
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket=U
.
∎

2.5.
Equational theories on
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}

The PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
quotients raw circuits only by
structural
bookkeeping:
PROP coherence plus the coherence laws of control functors.
To reason about genuine circuit identities (e.g. moving phases, rewriting
controlled patterns, decomposing swaps), we will work with an additional
equational theory
\Gamma
on
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

It is useful to keep two notions of equality separate:

•

structural equality

C\equiv C^{\prime}
on raw syntax
(Definition
2.6
); and

•

content equality
generated by extra circuit equations in a chosen
equational theory
\Gamma
on
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

Definition 2.13.

An
equational theory

\Gamma
on
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
is a collection of well-typed
equations
f=g
between morphisms of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

For such a
\Gamma
, let
(\approx_{\Gamma})_{n}
be the least family of
relations on the hom-sets
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(n,n)
such that:

(1)

every axiom
f=g
in
\Gamma
satisfies
f\approx_{\Gamma}g
;

(2)

each
\approx_{\Gamma}
is an equivalence relation;

(3)

f_{1}\approx_{\Gamma}g_{1}
and
f_{2}\approx_{\Gamma}g_{2}
imply

f_{2}\circ f_{1}\approx_{\Gamma}g_{2}\circ g_{1}
;

(4)

f\approx_{\Gamma}g
and
h\approx_{\Gamma}k
imply

f\otimes h\approx_{\Gamma}g\otimes k
;

(5)

f\approx_{\Gamma}g
implies

\mathtt{C}_{k}(f)\approx_{\Gamma}\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)
for every
k\in[d]
.

We write
\Gamma\vdash f=g
to mean
f\approx_{\Gamma}g
.
When
C,C^{\prime}
are raw terms, the judgement
\Gamma\vdash C=C^{\prime}
is shorthand
for
\Gamma\vdash[C]_{\equiv}=[C^{\prime}]_{\equiv}
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.
Intuitively,
\Gamma\vdash f=g
is the congruence closure of
\Gamma
under
sequential composition, parallel composition, and adding controls.

Definition 2.14.

For each
d\geq 2
, let
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
be the equational theory on
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
generated
by the axiom
schemata
displayed in
Figures
1
and
2
.

We will show in Theorem
4.19
that
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
is sound and complete
for the unitary semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}\to\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}
.
Some schemata in Figures
1
–
2
use
derived-gate abbreviations, defined next in §
3.1
.
Having fixed
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
and its intended semantics, we can now state the finite set
of local schemata that axiomatise equality in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

3.
A Finite Axiom System for Qudit Circuits

Fix
d\geq 2
. This section presents the finite set of
local
axiom
schemata that generates our equational theory
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
on the circuit
PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
(Definition
2.7
). These schemata are the
non-structural rewrite principles used later in the completeness transfer of
Section
4
. Throughout, all equations are taken

in

\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
, i.e. modulo the PROP laws and the
control-coherence equations already built into
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

3.1.
Derived gates used in the axiom schemata

The generators of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
are the structural PROP circuits, the adjacent
two-level Hadamards

(
r\in[d-1]
), the scalar phases

(
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
), and the control constructors
\mathtt{C}_{k}(-)
.
The axiom schemata in Figures
1
–
2

are written using a small family of
derived
one-qudit gates
(and derived two-level Hadamards), matching the decoding notation of
§
4.4
.
These are purely
abbreviations
for composite circuits in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
:
they introduce no new generators; instead, they package common subcircuits so
that the schemata remain readable.

For readability, we present these derived gates in algebraic form in this
subsection only. Crucially, symbols such as
H^{(i,j)}
,
X^{(i,j)}
, and

R_{x}^{(i,j)}(\theta)
denote
morphisms in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
(i.e. circuit
diagrams modulo the structural congruence),
not
matrices. Only after
applying the interpretation functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}\to\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}
do they correspond to the intended unitary
operators. We write
H^{(r,r+1)}

for the adjacent Hadamard generator

.

We will use three families of derived one-qudit morphisms (all abbreviations in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
):

(1)

X^{(i,j)}
for
i,j\in[d]
;

(2)

H^{(i,j)}
for distinct
i,j\in[d]
;

(3)

R_{x}^{(i,j)}(\theta)
for distinct
i,j\in[d]
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.

:

For each adjacent pair
(r,r+1)
we define the adjacent swap
X^{(r,r+1)}
by

(6)

X^{(r,r+1)}:=H^{(r,r+1)}\circ\mathtt{C}_{r+1}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/piphase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\circ H^{(r,r+1)}

For notational uniformity, we set
X^{(i,i)}:=\mathrm{id}_{1}
. For distinct
i,j\in[d]
, we define the two-level swap
X^{(i,j)}
as a composite of adjacent swaps.
Assuming
i<j
, set
S_{i,j}:=\prod_{k=0}^{j-i-1}X^{(i+k,\,i+k+1)}
where
\prod_{k=0}^{m}f_{k}:=f_{m}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}
, then

(7)

X^{(i,j)}:=S_{i+1,j}^{-1}\circ X^{(i,i+1)}\circ S_{i+1,j},

and for
i>j
set
X^{(i,j)}:=X^{(j,i)}
.

:

We define the two-level Hadamard
H^{(i,j)}
on levels
(i,j)
by conjugating an adjacent Hadamard by the corresponding swap that “slides” the relevant level into position.
For
i=j
, set
H^{(i,i)}:=\mathrm{id}_{1}
.
If
i<j
, set

(8)

H^{(i,j)}:=X^{(j,i+1)}\circ H^{(i,i+1)}\circ X^{(j,i+1)},

and for
i>j
define the ordered version by swapping the two levels:

(9)

H^{(i,j)}:=X^{(j,i)}\circ H^{(j,i)}\circ X^{(j,i)}

:

Finally, for distinct
i,j\in[d]
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the two-level beam-splitter rotation
R_{x}^{(i,j)}(\theta)
by

(10)

R_{x}^{(i,j)}(\theta):=H^{(i,j)}\circ\mathtt{C}_{i}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\circ\mathtt{C}_{j}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/negphase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\circ H^{(i,j)}

Under
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
, these families act as follows:

•

\left\llbracket X^{(i,j)}\right\rrbracket
swaps
\left|i\right\rangle
and
\left|j\right\rangle
and fixes all other computational basis states.

•

\left\llbracket H^{(i,j)}\right\rrbracket
acts as the usual
2\times 2
Hadamard on
\mathrm{span}\{\left|i\right\rangle,\left|j\right\rangle\}

(in the ordered basis
(\left|i\right\rangle,\left|j\right\rangle)
) and as the identity on the orthogonal complement.

•

\left\llbracket R_{x}^{(i,j)}(\theta)\right\rrbracket
acts on
\mathrm{span}\{\left|i\right\rangle,\left|j\right\rangle\}
as

\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta)&\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta)\\
\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta)&\cos(\theta)\end{pmatrix}

(in the ordered basis
(\left|i\right\rangle,\left|j\right\rangle)
) and as the identity elsewhere.

To state several families of axioms systematically in
d
, we use a compact
meta-notation in circuit diagrams. An
indexed box
is a dash-dotted
rectangle surrounding a subdiagram. It is
not
part of the raw syntax of
Definition
2.5
; it abbreviates a finite iterated
composite of the enclosed circuit.

An indexed box is annotated by an index variable
k
together with an optional
unary predicate
\varphi(k)
, where
k
is the only free variable. The
intended reading is that
k
ranges over integers with
0\leq k<d
that
satisfy
\varphi(k)
(for instance
\varphi(k)
may be
a\leq k<b
, or

k\neq 0
). The box denotes the sequential composite of the enclosed
subcircuit, taken in increasing order of
k
:

\scalebox{0.83}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/bangbox-p.pdf}}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.83}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/bangbox-unfold.pdf}}}}}}}$}}.

If the predicate is omitted (we write just “
k
”), this abbreviates the
default predicate
0\leq k<d
.

For instance, if
d=3
, then

\scalebox{0.8}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/bangbox-example.pdf}}}}}}}$}}=\scalebox{0.8}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/bangbox-example-unfold.pdf}}}}}}}$}}.

Note that indexed boxes expand only by
sequential
composition along the same wires,
so they do not increase wire arity (all schemata still involve at most three wires).

3.2.
The axiom schemata and how to read them

This subsection fixes the axiom schemata generating
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
, and
explains the intended roles among them. The rules are presented
as local equalities between circuit diagrams; later, we use them to derive the
global control-algebra principles (compatibility, commutativity, and
exhaustivity) needed for the encoding/decoding arguments.

We define
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
as the congruence generated by the schematic
equations in Figures
1
–
2
and
closed under
\circ
,
\otimes
, and all
\mathtt{C}_{k}

(Definition
2.13
). As always, these are equations
in

\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
: they are stated modulo the PROP law and the control
functoriality/coherence already built into
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

A useful reading is that Figure
1
collects the
one- and two-qudit identities that form the main “gate algebra” of the
theory, while Figure
2
supplies the finite
gate-level ingredients from which the
global
control principles of
Definition
2.4
become derivable.

We split the non-structural axioms into two groups according to their role in
the development.

•

Core axioms
(Figure
1
) form the main algebraic
interface of the circuit theory: phase arithmetic, basic two-level identities,
the swap decomposition, and the local decomposition/rotation principles.

•

Control-algebra support axioms
(Figure
2
) are
included so that the global control-algebra principles—namely

compatibility
,
commutativity
, and
exhaustivity
from
Definition
2.4
—are
derivable inside

\mathrm{QC}_{d}
, rather than assumed as part of the underlying structure.

Concretely, one could strengthen the definition of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
by quotienting
the raw syntax additionally by compatibility, commutativity and exhaustivity
of control (or by taking these as global rewrite schemata). We deliberately
do not do this: these principles are semantically sound for the intended
projector-based control interpretation, but they are not consequences of
control functoriality alone. Keeping them out of the structural congruence
makes the ambient PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
conservative, and ensure that the
completeness theorem is genuinely a statement about what follows from the
finite axiom set
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

(Sum)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/two-phases.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase-sum.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(
2\pi
)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase2pi.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(XH)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/XH.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/HX.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(H
2
)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hiip-Hiip.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(HHd)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/HHcommA.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/HHcommB.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(HPd)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/HphasecommA.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/HphasecommB.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(HCd)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/HcnotA.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/HcnotB.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(EH)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/eulerHL.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/eulerHR.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(3Rx)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/3bsL.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/3bsR.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(3CRx)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/3bsaltL.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/3bsaltR.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(S)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/CX-XC-CX-bangbox.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}

Figure 1
.

Core axiom schemata for
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

In (
EH
), (
3Rx
) and (
3CRx
) the right-hand-side angles are
determined by the explicit relations recorded in Appendix
A
.
Rules (
XH
), (
HPd
), and (
HCd
) apply when the indices

i,j,k
are pairwise distinct.
In (
HHd
) the indices
i,j,k,\ell
are pairwise distinct.

Framed collection of circuit-diagram rewrite axioms defining the core rules of the equational
theory QC_d: phase arithmetic, basic two-level identities involving swaps, Hadamards and phases,
a swap decomposition into controlled operations, and the local decomposition/rotation schemata
(EH), (3Rx) and (3CRx). Each axiom is shown as an equality between a left-hand and a

)

Figure 2
.

Auxiliary axiom schemata for
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

(1) “Totalisation” rules (
TP
) and (
TH
),
used to recover exhaustivity of control by a structural induction on circuits.
If one takes exhaustivity as an admissible rule schema, then (TP) and (TH) are
immediate instances and can be omitted.
(2) Local commutation/conjugation schemata for
differently-controlled
operations:
(
PPc
), (
P

π

c
), (

⁢
π
π

c
), (

⁢
π
π

dc
),
(
HPc
), (
HCc
), (
HHc
) require
k\neq\ell
.
These patterns are redundant once one is allowed to use distinct-control commutativity as a derived rule.
(3) The mixed-control swap schemata (
CompP
) and (
Comp

π

).
If compatibility were assumed as an ambient
(structural) principle of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
, then (

) and (

) would be
