Framed collection of auxiliary circuit-diagram rewrite axioms for QC_d. It includes the two totalisation schemata
(TP) and (TH) used to derive exhaustivity of control, a finite set of local commutation and conjugation patterns for
operations controlled on different basis values (the cases with k not equal to l), and the additional compatibility
schemata (CompP) and (Comp\(\pi\)) that exchange nested controls up to swapping the control wires. Each schema is displayed
as an equality between two circuit diagrams.

3.3.
Derived control-algebra laws

The three control-algebra equations of Definition
2.4
are derivable in the circuit
PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
from the axiom system
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.
The proofs are purely syntactic and are deferred to
Appendices
C
,
D
and
E
.
The present subsection records the results and the common reduction step used throughout.

Lemma 3.1
.

Every morphism of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
is provably equal in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
to a circuit generated (using only sequential and
parallel composition) by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle=\{\mathrm{id}_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\mid\theta\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadij.pdf}}}}}}}$}\mid 0\leq i<j<d\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\mathtt{C}_{k}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\mid k\in[d],\theta\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{\mathtt{C}_{k}(\mathtt{C}_{\ell}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/piphase.pdf}}}}}}}$}))\mid k,\ell\in[d]\}.

Lemma
3.1
is obtained by a normalisation procedure that pushes controls down to
a bounded set of controlled “layers” (Appendix
C.1
).
Once circuits have been reduced to this form, each control-algebra statement can be proved by
tracking how the outer controls interact with layers from
\mathcal{G}
.
Compatibility reduces to exchanging the (unique) layers that simultaneously involve both outer control
wires (Appendix
C
); commutativity reduces to commuting pairs of differently-controlled
layers from
\mathcal{G}
(Appendix
D
); and exhaustivity is then proved by induction,
using the totalisation axioms together with the derived compatibility and commutativity steps
(Appendix
E
).

Theorem 3.2
.

For any
n\geq 0
, any
C:n\to n
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
, and any
a,b\in[d]
,

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\mathtt{C}_{a}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{b}(C)\bigr)\circ\bigl(\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n}\bigr)=\bigl(\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n}\bigr)\circ\mathtt{C}_{b}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{a}(C)\bigr).

Theorem 3.3
.

For any
n\geq 0
, any
C_{1},C_{2}:n\to n
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
, and any distinct
k,\ell\in[d]
,

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\mathtt{C}_{k}(C_{1})\circ\mathtt{C}_{\ell}(C_{2})=\mathtt{C}_{\ell}(C_{2})\circ\mathtt{C}_{k}(C_{1}).

Theorem 3.4
.

For every
C:n\to n
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
,

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(C)=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes C.

4.
Embedding Qudit Circuits into LOPP Circuits

Our completeness proof for
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
proceeds by reduction to linear optics.
We translate qudit circuits in the PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
into circuits in the linear-optical PROP
LOPP
,
appeal to the sound and complete equational theory
\mathrm{LOPP}
for
LOPP

(
7
;
18
)
,
and then transport derivations back to
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.
The bridge is a fixed reflected
d
-ary Gray ordering of the computational basis
(§
4.2
): it identifies the
d^{n}
optical modes with the
n
-qudit basis states in such a
way that
consecutive
modes differ in exactly one digit by
\pm 1
.
As a result, each local
LOPP
generator (acting on one mode or two consecutive modes)
decodes to either a basis-controlled phase or a (multi-)controlled adjacent two-level qudit gate.

A key technical point (already present for qubits
(
8
)
) is that, although both
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}

and
LOPP
are PROPs, their monoidal products have different semantics:

\otimes
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
is interpreted by tensor product on Hilbert spaces,
whereas
\otimes
in
LOPP
is interpreted by direct sum under the single-photon model
(Remark
1
).
Consequently, our translations preserve sequential composition
\circ
but cannot be strict monoidal PROP morphisms.
We therefore work on
raw
syntax and fix a deterministic serialisation of
\otimes
.

Concretely, we define two context-sensitive translations:

•

a
contextual encoding

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}
(Definition
4.8
) sending a raw
n
-qudit circuit
to a raw
LOPP
circuit on
d^{a+n+b}
modes, intended to represent the circuit acting on the

middle

n
qudits of an
(a+n+b)
-qudit register; and

•

a
contextual decoding

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}
(Definition
4.11
) sending a raw
LOPP
circuit
supported on consecutive modes
t,t+1,\dots,t+\ell{-}1\subseteq\{0,\dots,d^{n}{-}1\}
to a raw
n
-qudit circuit,
by interpreting each local optical generator as a suitably (multi-)controlled two-level qudit gate in Gray order.

The completeness transfer uses three ingredients:

(1)

Enc
(Lemma
4.10
):
\mathrm{E}
is semantics-preserving for the Gray-ordered
LOPP semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}
(Definition
4.7
);

(2)

Retr
(Theorem
4.17
): decoding the encoding recovers the original circuit (padded by identities),
provably in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
; and

(3)

Mimic
(Theorem
4.16
): any
\mathrm{LOPP}
derivation decodes to a
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
derivation.

Together with completeness of
\mathrm{LOPP}
, this yields completeness of
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

4.1.
The LOPP calculus as a semantic model

This subsection recalls the linear-optical circuit PROP
LOPP
and its standard
single-photon
unitary semantics.
These are the only facts about
LOPP
we use in the proof transfer: the raw syntax and quotient PROP,
the interpretation
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}
, and the existence of a fixed finite sound and complete equational theory.

Let
\Sigma^{\mathrm{LOPP}}
be the (typed) set of generators

\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:0\to 0,\qquad\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:1\to 1,\qquad\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:2\to 2,

together with the optical generators (each indexed by
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}:1\to 1,\qquad\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/beamsplitter.pdf}}}}}}}$}}:2\to 2.

Intuitively, these are the empty diagram, the identity wire, a swap of two adjacent modes, and the two basic
optical components: a phase shifter and a beam splitter.

Definition 4.1.

Let
\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}
be the free
endomorphism
PROP on
\Sigma^{\mathrm{LOPP}}
(so
\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(m,n)=\varnothing
for
m\neq n
).
Let
\equiv
be the least congruence on
\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}
containing all instances of the strict PROP equations
(identities, associativity/unit for
\circ
and
\otimes
, interchange, symmetry naturality and involutivity),
as in Definition
2.6
.

Define the endomorphism PROP
LOPP
by

\textbf{{LOPP}}(m,m):=\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(m,m)\big/\equiv,\;\textbf{{LOPP}}(m,n):=\varnothing\ \text{if }m\neq n,

with
\circ
and
\otimes
induced by the term constructors.

Thus, a morphism in
\textbf{{LOPP}}(m,m)
is an
m
-mode linear-optical circuit modulo strict symmetric-monoidal bookkeeping.

For
m\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{M}_{m}:=\mathbb{C}^{m}
with its standard
mode basis

\{\left|0\right\rangle,\dots,\left|m-1\right\rangle\}
.
We identify unitaries
U\in U(\mathcal{M}_{m})
with their
m\times m
matrices in this basis, i.e. with elements of
U(m)
.
For disjoint mode blocks of sizes
m
and
n
, parallel composition corresponds to the
block-diagonal direct sum
:
for
U\in U(m)
and
V\in U(n)
,

U\oplus V:=\begin{pmatrix}U&0\\
0&V\end{pmatrix}\in U(m+n),

using the canonical identification
\mathcal{M}_{m+n}\cong\mathcal{M}_{m}\oplus\mathcal{M}_{n}
.

Definition 4.2.

Working in the standard mode bases of
\mathcal{M}_{m}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
, we interpret every raw
LOPP
term on
m
modes
as an
m\times m
unitary matrix.

The
single-photon interpretation
of
LOPP
is the family of maps

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}:\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(m,m)\longrightarrow U(m)\qquad(m\in\mathbb{N})

defined by structural induction on raw terms:

•

Generators.

\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}:=1,\qquad\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}:=\bigl(1\bigr),\qquad\left\llbracket\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}:=\bigl(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\bigr),

\left\llbracket\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\[0.86108pt]
1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/beamsplitter.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta)&\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta)\\
\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta)&\cos(\theta)\end{pmatrix}.

•

Sequential composition.

For
C_{1},C_{2}\in\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(m,m)
,

\left\llbracket C_{2}\circ C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}:=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}\circ\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}.

•

Parallel composition.

For
C_{1}\in\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(m,m)
and

C_{2}\in\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(n,n)
,

\left\llbracket C_{1}\otimes C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}:=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}\oplus\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}.

The interpretation
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}
respects the structural congruence
\equiv
of Definition
4.1
,
hence factors through the quotient to a well-defined family of maps

\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}:\textbf{{LOPP}}(m,m)\to U(\mathcal{M}_{m})
,
which we again denote by
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}
.

Remark 1
.

Under the single-photon interpretation, the monoidal product of
LOPP
is
block-diagonal sum
:

\left\llbracket C_{1}\otimes C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}\oplus\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}.

In contrast, the monoidal product of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
is interpreted by the
Kronecker tensor product
:

\left\llbracket C_{1}\otimes C_{2}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket\otimes\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket.

Because
U\oplus V
and
U\otimes V
are fundamentally different operations,
our translations preserve sequential composition but must serialise
\otimes
;
they therefore cannot be strict monoidal PROP morphisms.

The LOPP signature contains no primitive notion of (poly)control; in our reduction, control arises only from
the Gray-coded identification between optical modes and computational basis states.

Theorem 4.3

(
(
7
;
18
)
)
.

Let
\mathrm{LOPP}
denote the equational theory consisting of the structural PROP equations together with the non-structural
axiom schemata of Figure
9
(listed explicitly in Appendix
I
).
Then
\mathrm{LOPP}
is sound and complete for the single-photon semantics:
for any circuits
C_{1},C_{2}:m\to m
,

\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{LOPP}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}.

Fix
d\geq 2
. Since an
n
-qudit system has Hilbert space dimension
d^{n}
, our encoding and decoding only use

LOPP
circuits on
d^{n}
modes. We write
\textbf{{LOPP}}_{d}
for the full subcategory of
LOPP
on objects

\{d^{n}\mid n\geq 0\}
. As noted in Remark
1
,
\textbf{{LOPP}}_{d}
is not closed under the monoidal product on objects,
but Theorem
4.3
applies
arity-wise
to each fixed object
d^{n}
.

4.2.
Gray codes for mode ordering

LOPP generators are local in the mode index: they act on one mode, or on two
consecutive
modes.
To decode such local optical steps as local qudit operations, we fix a Gray ordering of the
d^{n}
computational basis states.
In this ordering, consecutive mode labels correspond to basis words that differ in exactly one digit, by
\pm 1
.
We will use this neighbour property in §
4.4
to interpret two-mode LOPP generators as (multi-)controlled adjacent
two-level qudit gates.

Fix
d\geq 2
and write
[d]=\{0,1,\dots,d-1\}
.
An
n
-qudit register has Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{n}=(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
with computational basis

\{\left|x\right\rangle\mid x\in[d]^{n}\}
.
In the single-photon optical model, a
d^{n}
-mode interferometer acts on
\mathcal{M}_{d^{n}}
with basis
\{\left|t\right\rangle\mid 0\leq t<d^{n}\}
,
where
\left|t\right\rangle
means “one photon in mode
t
”.
We now choose an explicit identification between these bases that interacts well with consecutive mode indices.

We use the standard reflected
d
-ary Gray code, which specialises to the usual binary reflected Gray code when
d=2
.

Definition 4.4.

Let
[d]=\{0,1,\dots,d-1\}
. For each
n\geq 0
define a map

G_{n}^{d}:\{0,1,\dots,d^{n}-1\}\longrightarrow[d]^{n}

recursively by:

•

G_{0}^{d}(0)=\epsilon
;

•

for
n\geq 1
, write
0\leq k<d^{n}
uniquely as
k=q\cdot d^{n-1}+r
with
0\leq q<d
and
0\leq r<d^{n-1}
, and set

G_{n}^{d}(k):=\begin{cases}q\cdot G_{n-1}^{d}(r)&\text{if $q$ is even,}\\[1.72218pt]
q\cdot G_{n-1}^{d}(d^{n-1}-1-r)&\text{if $q$ is odd.}\end{cases}

Here
\epsilon
is the empty word, and
q\cdot w
denotes prefixing the word
w
by the digit
q
.
The next lemma records the operational fact we need: in Gray order, incrementing the mode index changes exactly one computational digit,
and only by an adjacent step.

For
d=3
and
n=2
the reflected Gray order is

00,01,02,12,11,10,20,21,22,

so successive words differ in exactly one digit, and that digit changes by
\pm 1
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For all
n\geq 1
, we have
G_{n}^{d}(0)=(0,\dots,0)
.
Moreover, for each
0\leq k<d^{n}-1
the words
G_{n}^{d}(k)
and
G_{n}^{d}(k+1)
differ in exactly one position,
and in that position the digit changes by one: if

G_{n}^{d}(k)=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\quad\text{and}\quad G_{n}^{d}(k+1)=(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}),

then their is a unique
t\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
a_{t}\neq b_{t}
and
|a_{t}-b_{t}|=1
, while
a_{j}=b_{j}
for all
j\neq t
.

We package the resulting basis reordering as a permutation unitary on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

Definition 4.6.

For each
n\geq 0
, define the unitary

\mathfrak{G}_{n,d}:\mathcal{M}_{d^{n}}\to\mathcal{H}_{n}
as the
permutation matrix

implementing the Gray reordering:

\mathfrak{G}_{n,d}:=\sum_{t=0}^{d^{n}-1}\bigl|G_{n}^{d}(t)\bigr\rangle\left\langle t\right|,

equivalently
\mathfrak{G}_{n,d}(\left|t\right\rangle)=\left|G_{n}^{d}(t)\right\rangle
for
0\leq t<d^{n}
.
In particular,
\mathfrak{G}_{n,d}^{-1}=\mathfrak{G}_{n,d}^{\dagger}
.

Operationally,
\mathfrak{G}_{n,d}
is the fixed change-of-basis map identifying the mode basis with the computational basis in Gray order.

Definition 4.7.

For each
n\geq 0
define a map on raw terms

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}:\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{n},d^{n})\longrightarrow\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(n,n)

by conjugating the single-photon matrix semantics with the Gray permutation matrix:

\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}:=\mathfrak{G}_{n,d}\circ\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}\circ\mathfrak{G}_{n,d}^{\dagger},

where
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}
is the single-photon interpretation of Definition
4.2
.

Thus
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}
is the single-photon semantics transported to the qudit computational basis using the fixed Gray identification.
For each
n\geq 0
, the map
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}
respects the structural congruence
\equiv
on
\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{n},d^{n})
,
and hence factors through the quotient to a well-defined function

\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}:\textbf{{LOPP}}(d^{n},d^{n})\longrightarrow\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(n,n).

Moreover, the family
(\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}})_{n\geq 0}
is compatible with identities and sequential composition, i.e. for all

C_{1},C_{2}:d^{n}\to d^{n}
in
LOPP
,

\left\llbracket\mathrm{id}_{d^{n}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}=I_{\mathcal{H}_{n}},\qquad\left\llbracket C_{2}\circ C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}\circ\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}.

Equivalently,
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}
is a well-defined functor
\textbf{{LOPP}}_{d}\to\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}
(with object map
d^{n}\mapsto n
).

Consequently, for
n\geq 0
and
C_{1},C_{2}:d^{n}\to d^{n}
in
LOPP
, if

\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}
in
\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}(n,n)
,
then
\mathrm{LOPP}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}
.

4.3.
Contextual encoding of quantum circuits

Fix
d\geq 2
.
This subsection defines the
contextual encoding

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}:\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)\to\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{a+n+b},d^{a+n+b})
,
which translates an
n
-qudit circuit into a single-photon linear-optical circuit on
d^{a+n+b}
modes.
The parameters
(a,b)
describe surrounding context:
\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(C)
is intended to represent
C
acting on the

middle

n
qudits of an
(a+n+b)
-qudit register.

We work on
raw
syntax, and we interpret
LOPP
circuits on
d^{n}
modes via the Gray-ordered semantics

\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}
of Definition
4.7
.
As noted in Remark
1
, the translation preserves sequential composition
\circ
but must
serialise

\otimes
.

The encoding uses three kinds of operations on raw
LOPP
syntax:
reordering Gray-ordered mode blocks to place an encoded subcircuit in context;
lifting a local
d^{n}
-mode circuit across surrounding Gray branches; and
lifting only along a designated branch to model control.
Because reflected Gray order reverses the traversal of blocks with odd leading digit,
the lifting gadgets sometimes mirror a local subcircuit; this is built into the constructions below.

We therefore fix the following raw
LOPP
constructions (defined explicitly in Appendix
G

and Section
J
):

•

For all
a,n,b\geq 0
, a raw permutation circuit

\sigma^{d}_{a,n,b}\in\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{a+n+b},d^{a+n+b}),

intended to implement, at the level of Gray-ordered mode labels, the block symmetry that swaps the middle
n
-qudit block
with the lower
b
-qudit block.

•

For all
p,n\geq 0
, an operator

\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{p}(-):\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{n},d^{n})\longrightarrow\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{n+p},d^{n+p}),

which lifts a
d^{n}
-mode circuit uniformly across the
d^{p}
Gray blocks determined by
p
surrounding qudits.

•

For all
p,n\geq 0
and
j\in[d]
, an operator

j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{p}(-):\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{n},d^{n})\longrightarrow\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{n+1+p},d^{n+1+p}),

which performs the same lift, but only on the branch where a designated control digit equals
j
(and acts as the identity on the other branches).

In the main text we use only how these gadgets interact with decoding, stated in Lemmas
4.14

and
4.15
.

Remark 2
.

Under the single-photon semantics,
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{p}(C)
is (up to Gray-induced permutations of the mode basis)
a block-diagonal direct sum of
d^{p}
copies of
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}
, one for each fixed choice of the surrounding
p
Gray digits.
The reflected Gray traversal reverses some suffix blocks (precisely when the prefix digit is odd); the GrayLift implementation
accounts for this by conjugating the affected blocks by a mode-reversal permutation.

Definition 4.8.

Fix
d\geq 2
. For each
n,a,b\geq 0
define a function

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}:\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)\longrightarrow\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{a+n+b},d^{a+n+b})

by structural induction on raw circuits.

(1)

Sequential composition.

For
C_{1},C_{2}\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
,

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(C_{2}\circ C_{1}):=\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(C_{2})\circ\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(C_{1}).

(2)

Parallel composition.

Let
C_{1}\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n_{1},n_{1})
and
C_{2}\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n_{2},n_{2})
, and view
C_{1}\otimes C_{2}
as acting on
n_{1}+n_{2}
qudits
with
C_{1}
on the upper block and
C_{2}
on the lower block. Set

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(C_{1}\otimes C_{2}):=\mathrm{E}^{a+n_{1}}_{b}(C_{2})\circ\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b+n_{2}}(C_{1}).

This fixes our serialisation convention: we encode the lower block first and then the upper block, matching the decoding convention of
Definition
4.11
.

(3)

Identities.

For the canonical identity
\mathrm{id}_{n}\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
, set

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(\mathrm{id}_{n}):=\mathrm{id}_{d^{a+n+b}}.

(4)

Swap.

For the swap generator, set

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}\bigl(\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\bigr):=\sigma^{d}_{a,b,2}\circ\sigma^{d}_{a+b,1,1}\circ\sigma^{d}_{a,2,b}.

(5)

Global phases.

For the scalar phase generator with parameter
\theta
, set

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}\bigl(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\bigr):=\left(\scalebox{1}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right)^{\otimes d^{a+b}}.

(Under the single-photon semantics, applying the same phase shifter on every mode yields a global phase.)

(6)

Two-level Hadamards.

For each
0\leq i<d-1
, fix a
d
-mode
LOPP
circuit

B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}:1^{\otimes d}\to 1^{\otimes d}

whose single-photon semantics is
H_{d}^{(i,i+1)}
.
(Existence follows from standard linear-optical decompositions; a concrete choice is given in Appendix
G
.)
Define

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$}\right):=\sigma^{d}_{a,1,b}\circ\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{a+b}\left(B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}\right)\circ\sigma^{d}_{a,b,1}.

(7)

Control.

For
C\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
and
j\in[d]
, define

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}\left(\mathtt{C}_{j}(C)\right):=\sigma^{d}_{a,n+1,b}\circ j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{a+b}\left(\mathrm{E}^{0}_{0}(C)\right)\circ\sigma^{d}_{a,b,n+1}.

Finally, for
C\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
we abbreviate
\mathrm{E}(C):=\mathrm{E}^{0}_{0}(C)\in\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{n},d^{n})
.

We record two basic properties of the encoding. First, it respects the structural congruence on raw qudit circuits and therefore descends to the quotient PROP.

Lemma 4.9
.

Fix
d\geq 2
. For all
n,a,b\geq 0
and all raw circuits
C,C^{\prime}\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
, if
C\equiv C^{\prime}
in the structural congruence of
Definition
2.6
, then

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(C)\ \equiv\ \mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(C^{\prime})

in the strict-PROP congruence on raw
LOPP
circuits of Definition
4.1
. Hence
\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}
induces a well-defined function

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}:\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}(n,n)\longrightarrow\textbf{{LOPP}}(d^{a+n+b},d^{a+n+b}).

Second, the encoding preserves the intended unitary semantics, where
LOPP
circuits are interpreted through the Gray identification.

Lemma 4.10
.

For every
n\geq 0
and every circuit
C:n\to n
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
,

(
Enc
)

\left\llbracket\mathrm{E}(C)\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket.

4.4.
Contextual decoding of LOPP circuits

This subsection defines a syntax-directed
decoding
map from raw
LOPP
circuits back to raw qudit circuits.
The key point is locality:

acts on one mode, while

and

act on two
consecutive
modes.
Under the reflected Gray ordering (Definition
4.4
), two consecutive modes correspond to computational basis words that differ in
exactly one digit by
\pm 1
(Lemma
4.5
).
Decoding therefore produces either a basis-state phase, or an adjacent two-level single-qudit gate controlled on the surrounding digits.

We reuse the derived one-qudit gate families

(two-level swaps) and

(two-level beam-splitter rotations) from §
3.1
.
Recall also that for a word
u\in[d]^{*}
and a circuit
f
we write
\mathtt{C}_{u}(f)
for iterated control by
u
.

Given
n\geq 1
and words
u,w\in[d]^{*}
with
|u|+1+|w|=n
, we define for any one-qudit gate
g:1\to 1
the
n
-qudit circuit

\Lambda^{u}_{w}(g):=\sigma_{|u|,|w|,1}\circ\mathtt{C}_{uw}(g)\circ\sigma_{|u|,1,|w|},

where
uw
is concatenation and
\sigma_{a,b,c}:=\mathrm{id}_{a}\otimes\sigma_{b,c}
.
Semantically,
\Lambda^{u}_{w}(g)
applies
g
to qudit
|u|+1
if and only if the surrounding qudits are in basis state

\left|u\right\rangle\otimes\left|w\right\rangle
, and acts as the identity otherwise.

Definition 4.11.

Fix
d\geq 2
and
n\geq 0
. For integers
t,\ell
with
0\leq t
and
t+\ell\leq d^{n}
, define a map

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}:\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(\ell,\ell)\longrightarrow\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)

by structural induction on the raw
LOPP
term, viewing it as occupying the consecutive global modes
t,\dots,t+\ell-1
:

(1)

Sequential composition.

If
C_{1},C_{2}\in\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(\ell,\ell)
, then

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{2}\circ C_{1}):=\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{2})\circ\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{1}).

(2)

Parallel composition (serialised).

If
C_{1}\in\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{1})
and
C_{2}\in\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(\ell_{2},\ell_{2})
, placed side by side so that
C_{1}
occupies modes

t,\dots,t+\ell_{1}-1
and
C_{2}
occupies modes
t+\ell_{1},\dots,t+\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-1
, then

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{1}\otimes C_{2}):=\mathrm{D}^{t+\ell_{1}}_{n}(C_{2})\circ\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{1}).

(3)

Structural generators.

For the empty diagram and the identity wire,

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}\left(\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram_lov.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right):=\mathrm{id}_{n},\qquad\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}):=\mathrm{id}_{n}.

(4)

Phase shifters.

Let

act on the (global) mode
t
, and write
G_{n}^{d}(t)=u\in[d]^{n}
. Define

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}\left(\scalebox{1}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right):=\mathtt{C}_{u}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\right).

(5)

Two-mode generators on consecutive modes.

Assume
n\geq 1
and consider a generator acting on consecutive global modes
t
and
t+1
(so
t+1<d^{n}
).
By Lemma
4.5
there are unique words
u,w\in[d]^{*}
, a unique digit
v\in[d]
,
and a unique sign
\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}
(with
v+\varepsilon\in[d]
) such that

G_{n}^{d}(t)=u\,v\,w,\quad G_{n}^{d}(t+1)=u\,(v+\varepsilon)\,w,\quad|u|+1+|w|=n.

•

Swap of modes.

For the
LOPP
swap generator, set

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}\left(\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right):=\Lambda^{u}_{w}\left(\scalebox{0.8}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Xvveps.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right).

•

Beam splitter.

For the
LOPP
beam splitter generator, set

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}\left(\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/beamsplitter.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right):=\Lambda^{u}_{w}\left(\scalebox{0.8}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Rxvveps.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right).

Finally, for a raw
LOPP
circuit
C
on
d^{n}
modes we abbreviate
\mathrm{D}(C):=\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)\in\textbf{{CQC}}^{\textup{raw}}_{d}(n,n)
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Each decoded generator acts non-trivially on at most one two-dimensional computational subspace (determined by one or two consecutive Gray indices)
and as the identity on the orthogonal complement:

•

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}\left(\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right)
applies a phase only on the basis vector
\left|G_{n}^{d}(t)\right\rangle
;

•

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}\left(\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right)
and
\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}\left(\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/beamsplitter.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right)
act only on

\mathrm{span}\{\left|G_{n}^{d}(t)\right\rangle,\left|G_{n}^{d}(t+1)\right\rangle\}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}
be raw
LOPP
circuits on
d^{n}
modes. If
C_{1}\equiv C_{2}
in the structural congruence generated by the strict PROP equations
(as in Definition
4.1
), then

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\ \vdash\ \mathrm{D}(C_{1})=\mathrm{D}(C_{2}).

Proof.

See Appendix
I
. The proof is by induction on strict-PROP
contexts.
The only non-trivial case is the mixed-product axiom; it is handled because decoding of subcircuits on disjoint mode blocks yields
(multi-)controlled gates that commute in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
(using derived distinct-control commutativity).
∎

We now record the two interface facts about the
LOPP
gadgets used in the encoding (introduced in §
4.3
)
that we will need for the encoding/decoding retraction argument.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}
be the contextual decoding map of Definition
4.11
.
For every
n,p\geq 0
and every raw
LOPP
circuit
C\in\textbf{{LOPP}}^{\textup{raw}}(d^{n},d^{n})
we have

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+p}\left(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{p}(C)\right)=\mathrm{id}_{p}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C).

Moreover, for each
j\in[d]
,

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+p}\left(j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{p}(C)\right)=\mathrm{id}_{p}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}\left(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)\right).

Proof.

See Appendix
H
, in particular Lemmas
H.4
and
H.5
.
∎

Remark 3
.

Intuitively, decoding a GrayLift produces a product over all control branches.
Regrouping branches uses derived commutativity of distinct control (Appendix
D
),
and collapsing the total product uses derived exhaustivity (Appendix
E
).

Lemma 4.15
.

For all
a,n,b\geq 0
,

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\mathrm{D}^{0}_{a+n+b}\left(\sigma^{d}_{a,n,b}\right)=\sigma_{a,n,b},

where
\sigma_{a,n,b}:a+n+b\to a+b+n
denotes the corresponding qudit-wire symmetry

\sigma_{a,n,b}:=\mathrm{id}_{a}\otimes\sigma_{n,b}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
J
.
∎

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
n\geq 0
and let
C_{1},C_{2}:d^{n}\to d^{n}
be circuits in
LOPP
. If

\,\mathrm{LOPP}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2},

then

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\mathrm{D}(C_{1})=\mathrm{D}(C_{2}).

Proof.

This follows by induction on
\mathrm{LOPP}
-derivations, using that decoding is compatible with the strict PROP congruence
(Lemma
4.13
) and by checking, axiom-by-axiom, that each non-structural
LOPP
axiom decodes to a derivable
equation in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
. Full details are given in Appendix
I
.
∎

Thus every rewrite used in an
\mathrm{LOPP}
derivation can be replayed, after decoding, inside
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.
We will combine this mimicking step with the encoding/decoding retraction in the next subsection to transfer completeness from
\mathrm{LOPP}
to
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

4.5.
Completeness via the linear-optical reduction

This subsection assembles the translation results into a single completeness argument for
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.
The main point is that encoding into
LOPP
and then decoding back is a retraction provable in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
;
combined with mimicking (Theorem
4.16
) and completeness of
\mathrm{LOPP}
,
this transfers semantic equality to derivability in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

Theorem 4.17
.

For all
n,a,b\geq 0
and all circuits
C:n\to n
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
,

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\mathrm{D}^{0}_{a+n+b}\left(\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(C)\right)=\mathrm{id}_{a}\otimes C\otimes\mathrm{id}_{b}.

Proof.

Pick a raw representative of
C
and argue by structural induction.
The cases for
\circ
and the (serialised)
\otimes
follow immediately from Definitions
4.8
and
4.11
.
All generator cases are handled uniformly by eliminating the block-permutation wrapper via Lemma
4.15
,
and discharging the remaining GrayLift steps via Lemma
4.14
; the full bookkeeping is given in Appendix
H
.

We only expand the key control step. Let
C=\mathtt{C}_{j}(C^{\prime})
with
C^{\prime}:m\to m
.
By Definition
4.8
we have

\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(C)=\sigma^{d}_{a,m+1,b}\circ j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}^{a+b}_{d}\!\bigl(\mathrm{E}^{0}_{0}(C^{\prime})\bigr)\circ\sigma^{d}_{a,b,m+1}.

Applying
\mathrm{D}^{0}_{a+m+1+b}
and using Lemma
4.15
(on the outer permutations)
together with the controlled GrayLift interface in Lemma
4.14
yields, in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{0}_{a+m+1+b}\bigl(\mathrm{E}^{a}_{b}(\mathtt{C}_{j}(C^{\prime}))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{a,m+1,b}\circ\bigl(\mathrm{id}_{a+b}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{m}(\mathrm{E}^{0}_{0}(C^{\prime})))\bigr)

\displaystyle\circ\sigma_{a,b,m+1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}_{a}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{m}(\mathrm{E}^{0}_{0}(C^{\prime})))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{b}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}_{a}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}(C^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{b},

where the middle equality is strict PROP coherence and the last equality uses the induction hypothesis for
C^{\prime}
at context
(0,0)
.
∎

Corollary 4.18
.

For every
n\geq 0
and every circuit
C:n\to n
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
,

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\ \vdash\ \mathrm{D}(\mathrm{E}(C))=C.

Proof.

Set
a=b=0
in Theorem
4.17
.
∎

Theorem 4.19
.

For every
d\geq 2
the equational theory
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
is sound and complete for
d
-dimensional unitary circuit.
That is, for any
n
-qudit circuits
C_{1},C_{2}:n\to n
we have

\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}.

Proof.

Soundness is immediate: each axiom schema is valid under
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
, and
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
is a strict symmetric monoidal functor
(Proposition
2.10
).

For completeness, if
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket
then by Lemma
4.10
we have

\left\llbracket\mathrm{E}(C_{1})\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}=\left\llbracket\mathrm{E}(C_{2})\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{LOPP}}
, hence completeness of
\mathrm{LOPP}
gives

\mathrm{LOPP}\vdash\mathrm{E}(C_{1})=\mathrm{E}(C_{2})
.
Decode and mimic (Theorem
4.16
) to obtain

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{E}(C_{1}))=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{E}(C_{2}))
, and conclude by Corollary
4.18
.
∎

5.
Conclusion

We presented a dimension-uniform, circuit-level equational theory for unitary qudit circuits.
Our starting point is the polycontrolled circuit PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
, where control is primitive:
for each
k\in[d]
, the constructor
\mathtt{C}_{k}(-)
functorially adjoins a distinguished control wire
and applies a circuit exactly when that wire is in basis state
\left|k\right\rangle
.
Over a fixed universal gate signature (adjacent two-level Hadamards, basis-controlled phases,
and the structural PROP generator), we defined
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
: a
finite
family of local axiom
schemata whose instances involve at most three wires and whose diagrammatic shape is
independent of
d
.
Our main theorem establishes soundness and completeness for the standard semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}\to\textbf{{Qudit}}_{d}
:

\mathrm{QC}_{d}
derives exactly the valid equalities of
exact
unitary semantics, including global phases as
0\to 0
scalars.
A notable consequence is that the expected
global
control algebra (compatibility, distinct-control commutativity, and exhaustivity)
is derivable within
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
, rather than being imposed as part of the ambient structural congruence.

Completeness is obtained by a modular proof transfer to linear optics.
Using the reflected
d
-ary Gray code, we encode
n
-qudit circuits as
d^{n}
-mode single-photon

LOPP
circuits (up to conjugation by a fixed Gray-basis permutation), and we handle the
tensor/direct-sum mismatch by a deterministic serialisation of
\otimes
.
We then prove an encoding/decoding retraction inside
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
and a mimicking theorem showing
that every LOPP rewrite step decodes to a derivation in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.
In this way, completeness of LOPP yields completeness of
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

Several directions remain open.
First, it is natural to extend beyond unitary endomorphisms to circuits with ancilla
initialisation, discard, and measurement, aiming for finitary, bounded-arity axiomatisations of
qudit
channels
.
Second, on the algorithmic side, it would be valuable to extract practical rewriting methods
from
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
(normal forms, decision procedures, or terminating/confluent fragments) and to
connect them to certified optimisation and verification pipelines for native qudit hardware.
Finally, exploring alternative embeddings or gate signatures that better align monoidal structure, may simplify both the
metatheory and its compiler-facing interfaces.

Appendix A
Angle relations for rotation axioms

This appendix fixes the
meta-level parameter functions
used in the
rotation axioms (EH), (3Rx) and (3CRx).
Each of these axioms is a schematic equation in which the
left-hand side

contains freely chosen real parameters, while the
right-hand side

parameters are
deterministic functions
of the left-hand side.
The purpose of recording these functions is purely bookkeeping:
it makes each axiom schema fully explicit, and (when needed) supports a
uniqueness convention for rewriting.

In Delorme’s one-qubit Euler parametrisations, the decomposition of a
two-level unitary naturally comes with an overall scalar factor
(
global phase
in
U(2)
).
In the present paper we apply such decompositions
inside a fixed
two-dimensional subspace

\mathrm{span}\{\left|i\right\rangle,\left|j\right\rangle\}
of a
d
-level system.
A phase factor that is “global” for the
2\times 2
block is
not
a scalar
on the full
d
-dimensional space: rather, it is the operation that multiplies
both
\left|i\right\rangle
and
\left|j\right\rangle
by the same phase while leaving the other basis
levels unchanged.
In our syntax this is realised by applying the same level-phase on both levels,
i.e. by
\mathtt{C}_{i}(\alpha)\circ\mathtt{C}_{j}(\alpha)
.
Crucially, on the
(i,j)
-subspace this acts as
e^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}I_{2}
, hence it

commutes
with every gate supported on
(i,j)
(in particular with

H^{(i,j)}
and with all level phases).
As a result, whenever the qubit formulas isolate a scalar phase, we are free to
slide the corresponding two-level phase and absorb it into neighbouring level
phases. This is the only reason our explicit angle formulas do not coincide
syntactically with the qubit case; semantically they implement the same
underlying
U(2)
parametrisation on
\mathrm{span}\{\left|i\right\rangle,\left|j\right\rangle\}
.

Throughout, equalities between angles are understood modulo
2\pi
.
We write
\arg(z)
for the principal argument of a non-zero complex number

z\in\mathbb{C}
, taking values in
(-\pi,\pi]
.
When we restrict an angle to a range (e.g.
\beta\in[0,2\pi)
), we always mean
its canonical representative in that interval.
We also use the standard two-argument arctangent
\operatorname{atan2}(y,x)

with values in
(-\pi,\pi]
.

Euler-type relation (EH)

The axiom (EH) is a two-level Euler-type identity (applied in the paper on a
subspace
\mathrm{span}\{\left|i\right\rangle,\left|j\right\rangle\}
of a qudit).
Syntactically, the left-hand side is parameterised by two real angles

\alpha_{0},\alpha_{2}
, while the right-hand side uses four angles

\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}
.
The intended meaning is:

for every choice of
(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{2})
, there is a
unique
choice of

(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3})
in the prescribed ranges that makes (EH)
sound (on the relevant two-dimensional subspace), and hence sound as a qudit
equation by acting as the identity outside that subspace.

We follow Delorme’s explicit Euler parametrisation (stated for qubits) and use
the same extraction function, with the “two-level global phase” convention
explained above.

Given
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, define

z=-\sin\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}}{2}\Big)+i\,\cos\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{2}}{2}\Big),\qquad z^{\prime}=\cos\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}}{2}\Big)-i\,\sin\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{2}}{2}\Big).

We then define
\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}\in[0,2\pi)
by cases.
The degenerate cases
z=0
or
z^{\prime}=0
are treated separately to avoid dividing
by
0
; the generic case uses the ratio
z/z^{\prime}
.

•

If
z^{\prime}=0
, set

\beta_{0}=2\,\arg(z),\qquad\beta_{1}=\frac{\pi+\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}}{2}-\arg(z),\qquad\beta_{2}=\frac{\pi+\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}}{2}-\arg(z),\qquad\beta_{3}=0.

•

If
z=0
, set

\beta_{0}=2\,\arg(z^{\prime}),\qquad\beta_{1}=\frac{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}}{2}-\arg(z^{\prime}),\qquad\beta_{2}=\pi+\frac{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}}{2}-\arg(z^{\prime}),\qquad\beta_{3}=0.

•

Otherwise (when
z\neq 0
and
z^{\prime}\neq 0
), set

\beta_{0}=\arg(z)+\arg(z^{\prime}),\qquad\beta_{1}=-\arg\Big(i+\lvert\frac{z}{z^{\prime}}\rvert\Big)+\frac{\pi+\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}}{2}-\arg(z),

\beta_{2}=\arg\Big(i+\lvert\frac{z}{z^{\prime}}\rvert\Big)+\frac{\pi+\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}}{2}-\arg(z),\qquad\beta_{3}=\arg(z)-\arg(z^{\prime}).

With this choice, Equation (
EH
) holds on the relevant two-dimensional
subspace. Since every occurrence of (EH) in the main development is applied to
a two-level gate supported on
\mathrm{span}\{\left|i\right\rangle,\left|j\right\rangle\}
and acts as the
identity on the other computational levels, these formulas can be used
systematically for all
d\geq 2
.

Three-rotation identities (3Rx and 3CRx)

The axioms (3Rx) and (3CRx) express the standard fact that the same real

3\times 3
rotation (hence an element of
\mathrm{SO}(3)
) admits both a

ZXZ
and an
XZX
Euler decomposition.
They are the qudit/circuit-side reflections of the “(E3)” principle used in
the linear-optical setting: (3Rx) is the uncontrolled identity, and (3CRx) is
its controlled analogue. The parameter relations are identical in both cases.

Concretely, let
R_{x}(\theta)
and
R_{z}(\theta)
be the standard real rotation
matrices about the
x
- and
z
-axes, and write

R_{E3}=\begin{pmatrix}r_{1,1}&r_{1,2}&r_{1,3}\\
r_{2,1}&r_{2,2}&r_{2,3}\\
r_{3,1}&r_{3,2}&r_{3,3}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{SO}(3).

The angles
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
(left-hand side of (
3Rx
)) and

\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3}
(right-hand side) are chosen so that

(11)

R_{E3}=R_{z}(\gamma_{1})\,R_{x}(\gamma_{2})\,R_{z}(\gamma_{3})=R_{x}(\delta_{1})\,R_{z}(\delta_{2})\,R_{x}(\delta_{3}).

Thus
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
are the
ZXZ
Euler angles of
R_{E3}

and
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3})
are the corresponding
XZX
Euler angles.

The extraction formulas below are exactly those used in the linear-optical
literature; they are stated with explicit “generic” and “gimbal-lock” cases,
since Euler angle are not unique in degenerate configurations.

Assume first that we are not in a gimbal-lock configuration for XZX, i.e.
-1<r_{1,1}<1
. Then

(12)

\delta_{2}=\arccos(r_{1,1}),\qquad\delta_{1}=\operatorname{atan2}(r_{3,1},\,r_{2,1}),\qquad\delta_{3}=\operatorname{atan2}(r_{1,3},\,-r_{1,2}).

The remaining cases correspond to gimbal lock for XZX, i.e.
\delta_{2}\in\{0,\pi\}
,
where only a constraint on
\delta_{1}\pm\delta_{3}
is determined:

•

If
r_{1,1}=1
, then necessarily
\delta_{2}=0
, and all solutions satisfy

(13)

\delta_{2}=0,\qquad\delta_{1}+\delta_{3}=\operatorname{atan2}(r_{3,2},\,r_{3,3}).

•

If
r_{1,1}=-1
, then necessarily
\delta_{2}=\pi
, and all solutions satisfy

(14)

\delta_{2}=\pi,\qquad\delta_{1}-\delta_{3}=\operatorname{atan2}(r_{3,2},\,r_{3,3}).

In gimbal-lock cases there remains one degree of freedom in

(\delta_{1},\delta_{3})
; any choice satisfying (
13
) or
(
14
) yields the same rotation matrix.
For definiteness one may choose canonical representatives by setting the last

X
-rotation to
0
.

Dually, if
-1<r_{3,3}<1
, then

(15)

\gamma_{2}=\arccos(r_{3,3}),\qquad\gamma_{3}=\operatorname{atan2}(r_{1,3},\,-r_{2,3}),\qquad\gamma_{1}=\operatorname{atan2}(r_{3,1},\,r_{3,2}).

The degenerate ZXZ cases are:

•

If
r_{3,3}=1
, then
\gamma_{2}=0
and all solutions satisfy

(16)

\gamma_{2}=0,\qquad\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{3}=\operatorname{atan2}(-r_{1,2},\,r_{1,1}).

•

If
r_{3,3}=-1
, then
\gamma_{2}=\pi
and all solutions satisfy

(17)

\gamma_{2}=\pi,\qquad\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{3}=\operatorname{atan2}(-r_{1,2},\,r_{1,1}).

Again one may choose canonical representatives (e.g. by setting the last

Z
-rotation to
0
), but any satisfying pair give the same rotation.

\mathrm{QC}_{d}
, and
write
\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}
when the circuit identity

C_{1}=C_{2}
is derivable from the axioms of
Figures
1
-
2

Proof of (
XInv
).

If
i=j
, this is trivial.

If
i<j

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Xij-Xij.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Xij-Xij-low1.pdf}}}}}}}$}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{Xiplusun-Xiplusun}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Xij-Xij-low2.pdf}}}}}}}$}\overset{\eqref{Xiplusun-Xiplusun}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Xij-Xij-low3.pdf}}}}}}}$}\overset{\eqref{Xiplusun-Xiplusun}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}

If
i>j
, by definition
X^{i,j}=X^{j,i}
so this is covered by the previous case.
∎

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hij-Hij.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hij-Hij-01b.pdf}}}}}}}$}\overset{\eqref{Xij-Xij}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hij-Hij-02b.pdf}}}}}}}$}

Proof of (

Appendix C
Compatibility

Fix
d\geq 2
and the polycontrolled dagger PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
of
§
2
, equipped with control functor
\mathtt{C}_{k}
for each

k\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
. In this appendix we prove
Theorem
3.2
(§
3.3
), i.e. the mixed-control

compatibility
principle (
Compat
in Definition
2.4
), inside
the equational theory
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

Our proof is purely syntactic and uses a normalisation step that will also be
reused in Appendix
D
(commutativity of distinct controls).
Throughout we work modulo:

•

the strict symmetric monoidal coherence in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}

(associativity, unit, symmetry, interchange), so wire permutations can be
freely inserted and removed; and

•

the coherence laws for each control functor
\mathtt{C}_{k}

(functoriality, strength, and naturality), so controls can be pushed
through structural contexts.

C.1.
Normalisation into controlled layers

Algorithm 1

Separate
: push parallelism out, isolate single-gate layers with explicit controls

1:

function

Separate
(
f
)

2:

let

f^{\prime}:=f

where we removed swaps using SMC coherence and (
S
)

3:

match

f^{\prime}

with

4:

case

f_{1}\otimes f_{2}
:

5:

return

(\,\textsc{Separate}(f_{1})\ \otimes\ \mathrm{id}_{\lvert f_{2}\rvert}\,)\ \circ\ (\,\mathrm{id}_{\lvert f_{1}\rvert}\ \otimes\ \textsc{Separate}(f_{2})\,)

6:

case

f_{1}\circ f_{2}
:

7:

return

\textsc{Separate}(f_{1})\ \circ\ \textsc{Separate}(f_{2})

8:

case

\mathtt{C}_{m}(f_{1})
:

9:

match

f_{1}

with

10:

case

g\circ h
:
return

\textsc{Separate}(\mathtt{C}_{m}(g))\ \circ\ \textsc{Separate}(\mathtt{C}_{m}(h))

11:

case

g\otimes h
:

12:

return

(\,\textsc{Separate}(\mathtt{C}_{m}(g))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert h\rvert}\,)\ \circ\ (\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert g\rvert,\lvert h\rvert})\ \circ

13:

return

(\,\textsc{Separate}(\mathtt{C}_{m}(h))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert}\,)\ \circ\ (\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert h\rvert,\lvert g\rvert})

14:

case

:
return

\textsc{Separate}(\textbf{Apply \ref{axiom-controlled-hadamard-decompose} to rewrite }f^{\prime})

15:

case

:
return

f^{\prime}

16:

case

\mathrm{id}_{n}
:
return

\mathrm{id}_{n+1}

17:

case

\mathtt{C}_{n}(g)
:

18:

match

g

with

19:

case

:
return

f^{\prime}

20:

case

:
return

\textsc{Separate}(\textbf{Apply \ref{axiom-ccp} to rewrite }f^{\prime})

21:

case

\mathtt{C}_{k}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phasealpha.pdf}}}}}}}$})
:
return

\textsc{Separate}(\textbf{Apply \ref{axiom-cccp} to rewrite }f^{\prime})

22:

otherwise

return

\textsc{Separate}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{m}(\textsc{Separate}(f_{1}))\bigr)

23:

otherwise
:
return

f^{\prime}

The point of this subsection is to isolate a small family of “one-gate
layers”, each equipped with an explicit control word. Concretely, we show
that every circuit can be rewritten (provably in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
) into a
sequential composition of such layers, with all wire permutations made
explicit. This normal form is what allows the compatibility argument to reduce
to the single axiom (C
\pi
S), and the commutativity argument to reduce to a
finite table of generator commutations.

Definition C.1.

An atomic layer is a morphism of the form

L:=(id_{p}\ \otimes\ G\ \otimes\ id_{q}):n\to n,\quad G\in\{id_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\mid\theta\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadij.pdf}}}}}}}$}\mid 0\leq i<j<d\}\cup\{\mathtt{C}_{k}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\mid k,\theta\}\cup\{\mathtt{C}_{k}(\mathtt{C}_{\ell}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/piphase.pdf}}}}}}}$}))\mid k,\ell\}.

A
controlled layer
is any circuit of the form
\mathtt{C}_{u}(L)

for a control word
u
.

If a circuit
C
is written as a sequential composition of controlled
layers

C=L_{m}\circ\cdots\circ L_{1},\qquad L_{t}=\mathtt{C}_{u_{t}}(id_{p_{t}}\otimes G_{t}\otimes id_{q_{t}}),

its
control-depth multiset
is

\mathsf{cd}(C)\ :=\ \bigl\{|u_{t}|\mid t=1,\dots,m\bigr\}.

We order such multisets by the usual multiset extension of the natural
order on
\mathbb{N}
.

We now give an explicit normalisation procedure. Informally, it:
(i) eliminates implicit swaps using strict monoidal coherence and the swap
decomposition axiom, (ii) pushes
\otimes
outward by serialising it into a
composition of “upper then lower” actions, and (iii) pushes controls down to
generators, expanding controlled composites using the bounded-arity axioms of

\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

Lemma C.2
.

Separate
terminates on all inputs
f
and returns a sequential composition

\textsc{Separate}(f)\ =\ L_{m}\circ\cdots\circ L_{1},

where each
L_{t}
is an atomic layer and all permutations appear only as explicit wire-swaps between such
layers.

Proof.

Define a well-founded measure on circuits by

\mu(f):=\bigl(\#\text{of non-identity gates in }f,\sum_{g\text{ gate in }f}\text{control-depth}(g)\bigr),

ordered lexicographically. Each recursive clause of the definition of

Separate
either:

•

eliminates a tensor node in favour of sequential composition of
smaller circuits (strictly reducing the number of non-identity gates
in the first component), or

•

pushes a control one step closer to a generator and immediately
expands a controlled generator using
Lemmas

CHDec
,

CCPDec
and
CCCPDec
, which strictly reduces the
total nesting depth of non-generator gates.

Functoriality, strength and swap laws are used only to rearrange the
diagram and do not increase
\mu
. Hence
\mu
decreases along every
recursive branch, so
Separate
terminates. By construction, the
output is a composition of controlled layers with generator drawn
from the stated finite set and with all permutations explicit.
∎

Corollary C.3
.

Every morphism of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
is provably equal in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
to a circuit
generated (using only sequential and parallel composition) by

\mathcal{G}=\{\,id_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\,\}\cup\{\,\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\mid\theta\in\mathbb{R}\,\}\cup\{\,\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadij.pdf}}}}}}}$}\mid 0\leq i<j<d\,\}\cup\{\,\mathtt{C}_{k}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\mid k,\theta\,\}\cup\{\,\mathtt{C}_{k}(\mathtt{C}_{\ell}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/piphase.pdf}}}}}}}$}))\mid k,\ell\,\}

C.2.
Compatibility of mixed controls

We now derive mixed-control compatibility (
Compat
in
Definition
2.4
) from the finite axiom system

\mathrm{QC}_{d}
, using the normalisation machinery above.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Write
S:=\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n}:2+n\to 2+n
.
Consider the two
(2+n)
-wire circuits

F_{ab}:=\mathtt{C}_{a}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{b}(f)\bigr),\qquad F_{ba}:=\mathtt{C}_{b}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{a}(f)\bigr).

Apply the normalisation procedure
Separate

(Algorithm
1
) to
F_{ab}
and
F_{ba}
.
By construction, each rewrite step performed by
Separate
is justified
either by strict PROP coherence, by control-functoriality/strength/naturality,
or by one of the bounded-arity axiom schemata of
Figures
1
–
2
.
Hence
\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash F_{ab}=\textsc{Separate}(F_{ab})
and

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash F_{ba}=\textsc{Separate}(F_{ba})
.

Inspecting the clauses of
Separate
, the
only
situation in which
two nested controls are left
undecomposed
is the branch

\textbf{case }\mathtt{C}_{m}(\mathtt{C}_{n}(g))\textbf{ with }g=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/piphase.pdf}}}}}}}$}:\ \textbf{return}{}f^{\prime},

i.e. nested control on the scalar
\pi
-phase.
All other nested-control cases are immediately expanded using
Lemmas
CCPDec
and
CCCPDec
(and the controlled-Hadamard
decompositions), so that in
\textsc{Separate}(F_{ab})
and
\textsc{Separate}(F_{ba})

the only atomic layers that can simultaneously involve the two
outer

control wires are instances of the doubly-controlled
\pi
-phase

\mathtt{C}_{a}(\mathtt{C}_{b}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/piphase.pdf}}}}}}}$}))
(possibly tensored with identity on untouched
wires).

We now push the swap
S
through the sequential decomposition

\textsc{Separate}(F_{ab})
from right to left.
For any layer that acts on at most one of the two outer control wires, this is
pure strict symmetric-monoidal coherence (naturality of
\sigma
).
For a layer equal to
\mathtt{C}_{a}(\mathtt{C}_{b}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/piphase.pdf}}}}}}}$}))
on the two outer control wires,
the required step is exactly the axiom schema (C
\pi
S),
i.e. (
CompP
).
Thus we can rewrite

\textsc{Separate}(F_{ab})\circ S=S\circ\textsc{Separate}(F_{ba})

in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.
Finally, replacing
\textsc{Separate}(F_{ab})
by
F_{ab}
and
\textsc{Separate}(F_{ba})
by
F_{ba}

using the equalities above yields
F_{ab}\circ S=S\circ F_{ba}
, as required.
∎

Appendix D
Commutativity

Fix
d\geq 2
and the polycontrolled dagger PROP
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
of
§
2
. In this appendix we prove
Theorem
3.3
(§
3.3
), i.e. that circuits controlled
on
distinct
computational-basis values commute.

As in Appendix
C
, we work modulo strict symmetric
monoidal coherences and the coherence laws of each control functor. We will use
the finite generating family
\mathcal{G}
obtained by the normalisation
procedure
Separate
(Corollary
C.3
) to reduce
the commutativity statement to a finite list of generator-level commutations.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

By Corollary
C.3
and SMC coherence we may assume that

f=id_{p}\otimes f^{\prime}\otimes id_{n-n_{f}-p},\qquad g=id_{q}\otimes g^{\prime}\otimes id_{n-n_{g}-q},

with
f^{\prime}:n_{f}\to n_{f}
and
g^{\prime}:n_{g}\to n_{g}
belonging to
\mathcal{G}
, and
where
0\leq p\leq q
.
If
q-p>n_{f}
, we can insert a symmetry

id_{p}\otimes\sigma_{n_{f},q-p-n_{f}}\otimes id_{n-q}
on both sides of
the desired equation to reduce to the case
q-p\leq n_{f}
.
Thus we only need to consider the relative offset

i:=q-p\in\{0,\ldots,n_{f}\}
.
If
g^{\prime}=id_{n_{g}}
then
\mathtt{C}_{\ell}(g)
is the identity and the
statement is trivial, so we exclude this case in the table below.

The remaining possibilities are summarised in the following table,
where each non-trivial entry cites the derived commutation lemma used
to swap the two layers in question (all equations are proved in
Appendix
B
).

: (
CH–CH≠
)

Each cited lemma states precisely that the corresponding pair of
controlled generators commutes for the indicated value of the offset

i
. Tensoring with identities on the untouched wires and inserting
coherence isomorphisms as needed yields

\mathtt{C}_{k}(f)\circ\mathtt{C}_{\ell}(g)=\mathtt{C}_{\ell}(g)\circ\mathtt{C}_{k}(f)

for each of the finitely many generator pair. Since arbitrary
circuits are built from these generators by sequential and parallel
composition, and
\mathtt{C}_{k}
is a strict monoidal functor, the general
case follows.
∎

Appendix E
Exhaustivity

In this appendix we prove Theorem
3.4
(§
3.3
),
i.e. that the family of control functors
(\mathtt{C}_{k})_{k=0}^{d-1}
is
exhaustive
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

Using the swap decomposition axiom (
S
) together with strict
symmetric-monoidal coherence, we may assume
f
is presented without explicit
swap generators. We then prove the claim by structural induction on
f
.

Let
\mathsf{E}(f)
denote the equation

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(f)=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes f.

We prove
\mathsf{E}(f)
for all circuits
f
by structural induction
on
f
using the generators and operations of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

Base cases.

For the monoidal unit and a single wire we have

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}})=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\qquad\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}})=\mathrm{id}_{2}=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},

so
\mathsf{E}(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}})
and
\mathsf{E}(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}})
hold. Since

\mathrm{id}_{n}\cong\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}^{\otimes n}
, the general identity case follows
from the tensor step below.

For a scalar phase

, the
total phase
axiom
(
TP
) gives

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}\bigl(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\bigr)\overset{\eqref{axiom-total-phase}}{=}\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$},

so
\mathsf{E}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})
holds.

For a two-level Hadamard

the

total Hadamard
equation (
TH
) yields

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}\bigl(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadij.pdf}}}}}}}$}\bigr)\overset{\eqref{axiom-total-hadamard}}{=}\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadij.pdf}}}}}}}$},

so
\mathsf{E}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadij.pdf}}}}}}}$})
holds.

Sequential composition.

Assume
\mathsf{E}(g)
and
\mathsf{E}(h)
.
By functoriality of each
\mathtt{C}_{k}
we have

\mathtt{C}_{k}(g\circ h)=\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\circ\mathtt{C}_{k}(h)
, hence

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(g\circ h)=\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\circ\mathtt{C}_{k}(h)\bigr).

Using commuting controls (
Theorem

3.3
), we can reorder the
factors so that all
\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)
appear to the left of all

\mathtt{C}_{k}(h)
:

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\circ\mathtt{C}_{k}(h)\bigr)\overset{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:commuting}}{=}\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\ \circ\ \displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(h).

By the induction hypothesis,

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes g,\qquad\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(h)=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes h,

and therefore

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(g\circ h)=(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes g)\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes h)=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes(g\circ h),

so
\mathsf{E}(g\circ h)
holds.

Parallel composition.

Assume
\mathsf{E}(g)
and
\mathsf{E}(h)
.
Using the definition of
\mathtt{C}_{k}
as a strict monoidal endofunctor
together with the strength/coherence laws, a controlled tensor can be
written (up to symmetry) as

\mathtt{C}_{k}(g\otimes h)=(\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert h\rvert})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert g\rvert,\lvert h\rvert})\circ(\mathtt{C}_{k}(h)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert h\rvert,\lvert g\rvert}).

Hence

\displaystyle\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(g\otimes h)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\Bigl((\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert h\rvert})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert g\rvert,\lvert h\rvert})\circ(\mathtt{C}_{k}(h)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert h\rvert,\lvert g\rvert})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:commuting}}{=}\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}(\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert h\rvert})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert g\rvert,\lvert h\rvert})\circ\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}(\mathtt{C}_{k}(h)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert h\rvert,\lvert g\rvert}).

Applying the induction hypothesis pointwise and using monoidality of

\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes{-}
,

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(g\otimes h)=(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert h\rvert})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert g\rvert,\lvert h\rvert})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\sigma_{\lvert h\rvert,\lvert g\rvert})=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes(g\otimes h),

so
\mathsf{E}(g\otimes h)
holds.

Nested control.

Finally, suppose
\mathsf{E}(g)
holds and consider
\mathtt{C}_{m}(g)
.
Using compatibility of mixed controls
(Theorem
3.2
),
nested controls may be exchanged at the cost of a swap:

\mathtt{C}_{k}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{m}(g)\bigr)=(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})\circ\mathtt{C}_{m}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\bigr)\circ(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert}).

Hence

\displaystyle\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{m}(g)\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})\circ\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{m}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\bigr)\circ(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})\circ\mathtt{C}_{m}\left(\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{k}(g)\right)\circ(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathsf{E}(g)}{=}(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert})\circ\mathtt{C}_{m}(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes g)\circ(\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\lvert g\rvert}).

By functoriality and coherence of
\mathtt{C}_{m}
, the right-hand side is
precisely
\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{m}(g)
, so
\mathsf{E}(\mathtt{C}_{m}(g))

holds.

Corollary E.1
.

Since we can write the swap gate using the generators above
(
Equation

S
),
\mathsf{E}(\sigma_{m,n})
also holds, and the previous lemma extends to all circuits in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

Since every circuit in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
is built from the above generators by
sequential composition, tensor product, and (nested) control, the
property
\mathsf{E}(f)
holds for all
f
, completing the proof.
∎

The exhaustivity property says that the collection of projectors

(\left|k\right\rangle\left\langle k\right|)_{k=0}^{d-1}
on the control wire forms a partition
of the identity, which is reflected syntactically by the equation of

Theorem

3.4
. As a useful consequence, controlled operations
are stable under conjugation on the target system.

\mathrm{QC}_{d}
, and
write
\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}
when the circuit identity

C_{1}=C_{2}
is derivable from the axioms of
Figures
1
–
2

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/XC-CX-XC.pdf}}}}}}}$}

∎

F.2.1.
A
\pi
-normalisation lemma (choosing the split parameter
x
)

In the proof of (
EulerB
) we introduce an auxiliary real parameter
x

(by splitting a middle rotation/phase into two pieces
x
and
\alpha_{2}-x
).
This produces a
family
of circuit identities indexed by
x
, in which
the angles
\beta_{i}(x)
and
\gamma_{i}(x)
produced by the Euler-extraction
depend on
x
(while the input angles
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
are fixed).

To continue the purely diagrammatic part of the derivation, we need one
additional property: we can choose a value
x_{0}
such that the

bridge phase

\beta_{3}(x_{0})+\gamma_{0}(x_{0})
is a multiple of
\pi
.
Since angles are always understood modulo
2\pi
, this means that

\beta_{3}(x_{0})+\gamma_{0}(x_{0})
is either
0
or
\pi
modulo
2\pi
, which is
exactly what justifies the two cases treated in the proof of (
EulerB
).

Lemma F.1

(Existence of a
\pi
-normalised split
(
32
)
)
.

Fix
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
and consider the continuous function

N:[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}]\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

N(x):=\sin(\alpha_{1})\cos(x)\cos(\alpha_{3})+\cos(\alpha_{1})\sin(\alpha_{3})\cos(\alpha_{2}-x).

Then their exists
x_{0}\in[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}]
such that
N(x_{0})=0
.

Moreover, in the Euler-extraction step used in the proof of (
EulerB
),
the corresponding bridge phase satisfie

\beta_{3}(x_{0})+\gamma_{0}(x_{0})\ \in\ \pi\mathbb{Z},

hence (modulo
2\pi
) we necessarily have

\beta_{3}(x_{0})+\gamma_{0}(x_{0})\equiv 0
or
\equiv\pi
.

Proof.

First note that

N\!\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\cos(\alpha_{1})\sin(\alpha_{3})\cos\!\left(\alpha_{2}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=-\cos(\alpha_{1})\sin(\alpha_{3})\sin(\alpha_{2}),

while

N\!\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\cos(\alpha_{1})\sin(\alpha_{3})\cos\!\left(\alpha_{2}-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\cos(\alpha_{1})\sin(\alpha_{3})\sin(\alpha_{2}).

Hence
N(-\frac{\pi}{2})=-N(\frac{\pi}{2})
, so

N(-\frac{\pi}{2})\cdot N(\frac{\pi}{2})\leq 0
.
By continuity of
N
, the intermediate value theorem yields

x_{0}\in[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}]
with
N(x_{0})=0
.

For the second part, recall how the angles
\beta_{3}(x)
and
\gamma_{0}(x)
are
obtained: they arise from solving the trigonometric matching conditions that
identify the two sides of the Euler-extraction step (equivalently: matching
the corresponding
2\times 2
blocks on the relevant two-dimensional subspace).
A standard calculation shows that the tangent of the bridge sum

\beta_{3}(x)+\gamma_{0}(x)
can be written as a ratio

\tan(\beta_{3}(x)+\gamma_{0}(x))=\frac{N(x)}{D(x)}

for some real-valued continuous denominator
D(x)

(the explicit shape of
D
is irrelevant here; it is the usual

t
-addition denominator and is continuous wherever the extraction is defined).
At
x=x_{0}
we have
N(x_{0})=0
, hence
\tan(\beta_{3}(x_{0})+\gamma_{0}(x_{0}))=0
, which
implies
\beta_{3}(x_{0})+\gamma_{0}(x_{0})\in\pi\mathbb{Z}
.
Reducing modulo
2\pi
gives the claimed dichotomy
\equiv 0
or
\equiv\pi
.
∎

Remark 4
.

The lemma only provides
existence
(not uniqueness) of a suitable
x_{0}
,
and any such choice is valid for the subsequent diagrammatic derivation.
Operationally, this is the only point in the proof where we appeal to a small
analytic argument; once
x_{0}
is fixed, the remainder proceeds purely by the
equational rules of
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

Proof of (
EulerB
).

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/eulerbF-02.pdf}}}}}}}$}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Commutativity},\eqref{sum},\eqref{Htotal}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/eulerbF-03.pdf}}}}}}}$}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{Hij-Hij}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/eulerbF-04.pdf}}}}}}}$}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eulerH}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/eulerbF-05.pdf}}}}}}}$}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Commutativity},\eqref{sum},\eqref{Htotal}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/eulerbF-07.pdf}}}}}}}$}

, so that

.

.
