Appendix G
Details of the GrayLift/Gray-Code Encoding

This appendix spells out two concrete raw-
LOPP
constructions used by the contextual encoding of
Section

4.3
:

(1)

an explicit
d
-mode
LOPP
circuit
B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}
whose single-photon semantics is the adjacent two-level Hadamard
H_{d}^{(i,i+1)}
; and

(2)

the GrayLift operators
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{p}(-)
and
j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{p}(-)
, which replicate a local
LOPP
subcircuit across Gray-coded mode blocks, inserting a mirror operation on blocks where the reflected
d
-ary Gray order runs the relevant suffix in reverse.

The main text use only the semantic interface properties of these gadgets (such as how they decode); the particular raw implementations here are simply one convenient choice.

G.1.
A single-qudit network for
H_{d}^{(i,i+1)}

Fix
d\geq 2
and
i\in\{0,\dots,d-2\}
.
Recall that in the encoding we need a local
LOPP
circuit on
d
modes which, under the single-photon semantics, acts like the qudit gate that mixes exactly two adjacent levels and leaves all other levels untouched.

Concretely, let

H:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\in U(2)

be the usual
2\times 2
Hadamard.
We define the adjacent two-level Hadamard
H_{d}^{(i,i+1)}\in U(d)
by

H_{d}^{(i,i+1)}:=I_{i}\oplus H\oplus I_{d-i-2}.

Thus
H_{d}^{(i,i+1)}
acts non-trivially only on the two-dimensional subspace
\mathrm{span}\{\left|i\right\rangle,\left|i+1\right\rangle\}
, and is the identity on all other computational basis vectors.
In particular:

•

when
d=2
and
i=0
, this is just the ordinary Hadamard
H
;

•

when
d=3
and
i=1
, it mixes modes
1
and
2
while leaving mode
0
unchanged.

Write
P(\phi):1\to 1
for the
LOPP
phase-shifter generator of angle
\phi
, and
B(\theta):2\to 2
for the
LOPP
beam-splitter generator of angle
\theta
(as in
Definition

4.2
).
We consider the following
2
-mode interferometer:

H_{\mathrm{BS}}:=(\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes P(-\pi/2))\circ B(\pi/4)\circ(\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes P(-\pi/2)):2\to 2.

Intuitively,
B(\pi/4)
is the standard
50/50
beam splitter (in our phase convention), and the surrounding phase shifters correct the relative phases so that the overall transformation is exactly the real Hadamard matrix in the mode basis.
This is the simplest way to implement a real
2\times 2
Hadamard using the complex-valued beam-splitter convention of
Definition

4.2
.

We then place
H_{\mathrm{BS}}
on modes
i,i+1
inside a
d
-mode circuit:

B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}:=\mathrm{id}_{i}\otimes H_{\mathrm{BS}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{d-i-2}:d\to d.

Operationally, if a single photon enters in mode
i
, then
B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}
outputs an equal-amplitude superposition over modes
i
and
i+1
; if it enters in mode
i+1
, the superposition has a relative minus sign; and all other modes are transmitted unchanged.

Lemma G.1
.

For each
d\geq 2
and
0\leq i\leq d-2
, the circuit
B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}
is a raw
LOPP
term built only from phase shifters, beam splitters, and identities, and its single-photon semantics satisfies

\left\llbracket B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}=H_{d}^{(i,i+1)}\ \in U(d).

Proof.

Under
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}
, the phase shifter
P(\phi)
acts as
[e^{\mathrm{i}\phi}]
on the corresponding mode, so

\left\llbracket\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes P(\phi)\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}=\mathrm{diag}(1,e^{\mathrm{i}\phi})
on
2
modes.
The beam splitter has matrix

\left\llbracket B(\theta)\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta)&\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta)\\
\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta)&\cos(\theta)\end{pmatrix}.

For
\theta=\pi/4
and
\phi=-\pi/2
we have
\cos(\pi/4)=\sin(\pi/4)=1/\sqrt{2}
and
e^{\mathrm{i}\phi}=e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/2}=-\mathrm{i}
, so

\left\llbracket H_{\mathrm{BS}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}=\mathrm{diag}(1,-\mathrm{i})\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot\mathrm{diag}(1,-\mathrm{i})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}=H.

Since parallel composition in
LOPP
is interpreted as block-diagonal direct sum on disjoint mode blocks, we have

\left\llbracket\mathrm{id}_{i}\otimes H_{\mathrm{BS}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{d-i-2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}=I_{i}\oplus H\oplus I_{d-i-2}=H_{d}^{(i,i+1)}.

∎

G.2.
Alternating mirror symmetry on Gray-coded modes

We now define the GrayLift gadgets used in the encoding, namely the operators
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{p}(-)
and
j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{p}(-)
introduced informally in
Section

4.3
.
Their role is easiest to understand by thinking in terms of Gray-coded blocks.

Fix
d\geq 2
and
n\geq 1
.
The reflected
d
-ary Gray code
G_{n}^{d}
(Definition
4.4
) orders the
d^{n}
basis words in
[d]^{n}
so that consecutive words differ in one digit by
\pm 1
.
This ordering has a crucial structural feature:
if we fix a prefix
u\in[d]^{n-1}
and vary only the last digit, the
d
words
u0,u1,\dots,u(d{-}1)
appear consecutively in Gray order, but they appear either in increasing order or in decreasing order depending on how the reflected recursion traverses that prefix.
For example:

•

for
d=2,n=2
, the reflected Gray order is
00,01,11,10
: the block with prefix
0
lists last digits as
0,1
, while the block with prefix
1
lists last digits as
1,0
;

•

for
d=3,n=2
, the reflected Gray order is
00,01,02,12,11,10,20,21,22
: prefixes
0
and
2
list last digits as
0,1,2
, while prefix
1
lists them as
2,1,0
.

In the encoding, we often want to take a local
LOPP
circuit
C
acting on a block of
d^{m}
consecutive modes (representing the Gray traversal of some suffix register) and apply that same logical action uniformly for every fixed configuration of some surrounding digits.
Because the Gray traversal flips direction on certain branches, “the same logical action” sometimes corresponds to applying
C
and sometimes to applying a mirrored copy of
C
, where the order of the modes inside that block is reversed.
The GrayLift operators implement exactly this systematic replication-with-optional-mirroring.

Definition G.2.

For each
r\geq 0
, let

\rho_{r}:1^{\otimes d^{r}}\longrightarrow 1^{\otimes d^{r}}

be the
LOPP
permutation circuit that reverses the order of the
d^{r}
modes:
on the single-photon basis
\{\left|0\right\rangle,\dots,\left|d^{r}-1\right\rangle\}
it acts by

\rho_{r}\left|k\right\rangle=\left|d^{r}-1-k\right\rangle
.

For any raw
LOPP
circuit
C:1^{\otimes d^{r}}\to 1^{\otimes d^{r}}
, define its mirror by

\mathtt{Rev}(C):=\rho_{r}\circ C\circ\rho_{r}^{-1}.

Since
\rho_{r}
is an involutive permutation,
\rho_{r}^{-1}=\rho_{r}
.
Equivalently, at the matrix level,
\mathtt{Rev}(C)
conjugates
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sp}}
by the reversal permutation matrix.

For
q\in[d]
, set

\mathtt{Rev}^{q}(C):=\begin{cases}C&\text{if $q$ is even},\\
\mathtt{Rev}(C)&\text{if $q$ is odd}.\end{cases}

This parity test matches the reflected recursion in the Gray code: odd leading digits reverse the traversal of the suffix.

Definition G.3.

Fix
d\geq 2
, let
m\geq 0
, and let
C:1^{\otimes d^{m}}\to 1^{\otimes d^{m}}
be a raw
LOPP
circuit.
For each
p\geq 0
define a raw
LOPP
circuit

\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{p}(C):1^{\otimes d^{m+p}}\longrightarrow 1^{\otimes d^{m+p}}

recursively by

\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{0}(C):=C,\qquad\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{p+1}(C):=\bigotimes_{q=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{Rev}^{q}\bigl(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{p}(C)\bigr).

Fix also
j\in[d]
.
Define the controlled GrayLift

j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{p}(C):1^{\otimes d^{m+1+p}}\longrightarrow 1^{\otimes d^{m+1+p}}

recursively by

j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{0}(C):=\bigotimes_{q=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{Rev}^{q}(C^{(q)}),\qquad C^{(q)}:=\begin{cases}C&\text{if $q=j$,}\\
\mathrm{id}_{d^{m}}&\text{if $q\neq j$,}\end{cases}

and

j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{p+1}(C):=\bigotimes_{q=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{Rev}^{q}\bigl(j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{p}(C)\bigr).

The recursion is best read as “one more surrounding digit”.
At level
p+1
, we split the
d^{m+p+1}
mode into
d
consecutive blocks of size
d^{m+p}
, indexed by the new leading digit
q\in[d]
.
On each block, we place a copy of the previous lift
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{p}(C)
, but mirrored when
q
is odd.

Two small expansions illustrate the pattern.
For
p=1
we have

\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{1}(C)=\mathtt{Rev}^{0}(C)\otimes\mathtt{Rev}^{1}(C)\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathtt{Rev}^{d-1}(C),

so for
d=2
this is
C\otimes\mathtt{Rev}(C)
, and for
d=3
this is
C\otimes\mathtt{Rev}(C)\otimes C
.

For
p=0
, the controlled lift is already informative:

j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{0}(C)=\mathtt{Rev}^{0}(C^{(0)})\otimes\mathtt{Rev}^{1}(C^{(1)})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathtt{Rev}^{d-1}(C^{(d-1)}),

so it places a single (possibly mirrored) copy of
C
on the
j
-th block and identities elsewhere.
For example, for
d=3
and
j=1
we get

j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{3}^{0}(C)=\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathtt{Rev}(C)\otimes\mathrm{id}
,
reflecting the fact that the
q=1
Gray branch is traversed in reverse order.

The next lemma isolates the combinatorial feature of Gray order that makes GrayLift correct: fixing a prefix produces a contiguous block of indices, and within that block the last digit runs either forward or backward.

Lemma G.4
.

Fix
d\geq 2
and
n\geq 0
.
For each word
u\in[d]^{n}
, they’re exist an index
s(u)
with
0\leq s(u)\leq d^{n+1}-d
and a sign
\varepsilon(u)\in\{+1,-1\}
such that

\{\,t\in\{0,\dots,d^{n+1}-1\}\mid G_{n+1}^{d}(t)\text{ has prefix }u\,\}=\{s(u),s(u)+1,\dots,s(u)+d-1\},

and for every
r\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
we have

G_{n+1}^{d}\bigl(s(u)+r\bigr)=\begin{cases}u\,r&\text{if $\varepsilon(u)=+1$,}\\
u\,(d-1-r)&\text{if $\varepsilon(u)=-1$,}\end{cases}

where
u\,r
denotes concatenation of the word
u
with the digit
r
.

Moreover, one may take
\varepsilon
to be the unique function
\varepsilon:[d]^{*}\to\{\pm 1\}
defined by
\varepsilon(\epsilon)=+1
and

\varepsilon(qu)=\begin{cases}\varepsilon(u)&\text{if $q$ is even},\\
-\varepsilon(u)&\text{if $q$ is odd}.\end{cases}

In particular,
\varepsilon(u)
flips sign exactly once for each odd digit encountered when reading
u
from left to right.

Proof.

We prove the block statement and the recursion for
\varepsilon
simultaneously by induction on
n
.

Base case
n=0
:
we have
u=\epsilon
and
G_{1}^{d}(t)=(t)
for
0\leq t<d
, so we may take
s(\epsilon)=0
and
\varepsilon(\epsilon)=+1
.

Inductive step:
let
n\geq 1
and write
u=qv
with
q\in[d]
and
v\in[d]^{n-1}
.
Any
t\in\{0,\dots,d^{n+1}-1\}
can be written uniquely as
t=q\cdot d^{\,n}+r
with
0\leq r<d^{n}
.

If
q
is even, then by
Definition

4.4
we have

G_{n+1}^{d}(qd^{n}+r)=q\cdot G_{n}^{d}(r)
.
Thus
G_{n+1}^{d}(qd^{n}+r)
has prefix
qv
iff
G_{n}^{d}(r)
has prefix
v
.
By the induction hypothesis, the set of such
r
is an interval of length
d
, hence so is the set of such
t
, with the same last-digit direction.
This corresponds to setting
\varepsilon(qv)=\varepsilon(v)
.

If
q
is odd, then
G_{n+1}^{d}(qd^{n}+r)=q\cdot G_{n}^{d}(d^{n}-1-r)
.
Put
r^{\prime}:=d^{n}-1-r
.
By the induction hypothesis, the set of
r^{\prime}
for which
G_{n}^{d}(r^{\prime})
has prefix
v
is an interval
[a,a+d-1]
.
Its image under
r=d^{n}-1-r^{\prime}
is the interval
[d^{n}-d-a,\,d^{n}-1-a]
, again of length
d
.
Since the map
r^{\prime}\mapsto d^{n}-1-r^{\prime}
reverse order, the last digit is traversed in the opposite direction on this block; this corresponds to setting
\varepsilon(qv)=-\varepsilon(v)
.
∎

The takeaway of
Lemma

G.4
is that each fixed prefix
u
determines a single contiguous mode block of length
d
in which only the last digit varies, and it varies either as
0,1,\dots,d-1
or as
d-1,d-2,\dots,0
.
This is exactly the reason the GrayLift recursion toggles mirroring when a leading digit is odd: it ensures that, once we identify modes with basis words in reflected Gray order, every lifted copy of a local subcircuit acts on its intended suffix register with the same logical orientation.

Appendix H
Encoding and Decoding

In this appendix we prove Theorem
4.17
(encoding/decoding retraction).
Recall that
E^{k}_{\ell}
is the contextual encoding (Definition
4.8
), and
\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}
is the contextual decoding (Definition
4.11
).
The theorem states that for every
C:n\to n
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
and every context size
k,\ell\geq 0
, decoding the encoded circuit recovers
C
padded by identities:

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\ \vdash\ \mathrm{D}^{0}_{k+n+\ell}\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(C)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes C\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}.

It is helpful to keep in mind what this statement is saying operationally.
The encoding
E^{k}_{\ell}
turns an
n
-qudit circuit into a
d^{k+n+\ell}
-mode single-photon optical circuit by using the reflected
d
-ary Gray ordering of computational basis states.
The decoding
\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}
goes the other way: it reads a local optical generator acting on one mode (a phase shifter) or two consecutive modes (a swap or a beam splitter) as a qudit operation supported on one two-dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, controlled on the surrounding Gray digits.
Because
\otimes
means tensor product on qudits but direct sum on optics, both translations are forced to be context-sensitive and to serialise tensor products.
The retraction theorem is the formal statement that these design choices are coherent: once the surrounding context is fixed, encoding and decoding cancel each other exactly (up to the expected identity padding on context wires).

The proof proceeds in three steps:

(1)

we analyse how
\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}
behaves on a contiguous Gray-code block corresponding to a fixed value of the most significant qudit;

(2)

we apply this to the alternating mirror-symmetry networks
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}
and
j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}
, which are the basic optical “lifting gadgets” used in the encoding;

(3)

we conclude by a structural induction on circuits in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

H.1.
Decoding a fixed Gray-code block

The key combinatorial fact is that, in reflected
d
-ary Gray order, mode indices are grouped into blocks according to the first Gray digit.
Fix
a\geq 0
. The
d^{a+1}
mode labels split into
d
consecutive blocks of length
d^{a}
:

\{0,\dots,d^{a}-1\},\ \{d^{a},\dots,2d^{a}-1\},\ \dots,\ \{(d-1)d^{a},\dots,d^{a+1}-1\},

and these blocks correspond to leading Gray digit
j=0,1,\dots,d-1
.
Inside the block for a fixed
j
, every Gray word has prefix
j
, so one expects every decoded generator to acquire an outer
\mathtt{C}_{j}
.
The only subtlety is that the reflected Gray code traverses odd blocks in reverse order.
For example, when
d=3
and
a=1
(so we are looking at words of length
2
), the reflected Gray order is

00,01,02,\ \ 12,11,10,\ \ 20,21,22,

so the
j=0
block is forward, the
j=1
block is reversed, and the
j=2
block is forward again.
The next two lemmas formalise exactly how decoding behaves in the forward and reversed cases.

Lemma H.1
.

Let
C
be a
LOPP
circuit acting on
\ell
consecutive modes, and fix

a\geq 0
and
s
with
s+\ell\leq d^{a}
. For every even

j\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
we have

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(C)\bigr).

Proof.

We argue by structural induction on
C
.
The intuition is that, for even
j
, the Gray code factors on that block as

G^{d}_{a+1}(d^{a}j+t)=j\cdot G^{d}_{a}(t),

so every decoded generator is the usual decoding at the smaller size
a
, wrapped in a
j
-control.

Empty and identity.
If
C
is the empty diagram

or a single wire

, then by definition of
\mathrm{D}
we have

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C)=\mathrm{id}_{a+1}=\mathtt{C}_{j}(\mathrm{id}_{a})=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(C)\bigr).

Phase shifter.
Suppose
C
is a phase shifter

on the mode with
global index
d^{a}j+s
.
Since
j
is even, the Gray code traverses the block with
leading digit
j
in increasing order, so

G^{d}_{a+1}(d^{a}j+s)=j\cdot G^{d}_{a}(s).

Decoding at offset
d^{a}j+s
therefore yields

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\right)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{G^{d}_{a}(s)}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\bigr)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\bigr).

As a concrete instance, for
d=3
,
a=1
,
j=0
and
s=1
we have
G^{3}_{2}(1)=01
and the decoded phase shifter is
\mathtt{C}_{0}(\mathtt{C}_{1}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}))
, i.e. the phase is applied only on the computational basis vector
\left|01\right\rangle
.

Beamsplitter.
Let
C=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/beamsplitter.pdf}}}}}}}$}
act on two consecutive modes

d^{a}j+s
and
d^{a}j+s+1
. Since
s+1<d^{a}
and
j
is even, we have

G^{d}_{a+1}(d^{a}j+s)=j\cdot G^{d}_{a}(s),\qquad G^{d}_{a+1}(d^{a}j+s+1)=j\cdot G^{d}_{a}(s+1).

Write
G^{d}_{a}(s)=u^{\prime}vw
and
G^{d}_{a}(s+1)=u^{\prime}xw
, so that

G^{d}_{a+1}(d^{a}j+s)=ju^{\prime}vw,\qquad G^{d}_{a+1}(d^{a}j+s+1)=ju^{\prime}xw.

In words, the two consecutive modes differ only in the
suffix
digit (the unique position where Gray neighbours differ); the leading digit stays equal to
j
.
Decoding

at offset
d^{a}j+s
therefore
produces exactly the same two-level gate as in the
a
-qudit decoding, but additionally controlled on the leading digit
j
:

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/beamsplitter.pdf}}}}}}}$}\right)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/beamsplitter.pdf}}}}}}}$})\bigr).

Swap.
The argument for the
LOPP
swap

is identical to the
beamsplitter case, using the decoding clause of
Definition
4.11
for swaps instead of beamsplitters.

Sequential composition.
If
C=C_{1}\circ C_{2}
(so the two subcircuits act on the same mode interval), then

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C)=\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C_{1})\circ\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C_{2})

by definition.
By the induction hypothesis,

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C_{t})=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(C_{t})\bigr)\quad(t=1,2),

so

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(C_{1})\bigr)\circ\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(C_{2})\bigr)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(C)\bigr),

using functoriality of
\mathtt{C}_{j}
.

Parallel composition.
If
C=C_{1}\otimes C_{2}
with
C_{1}
on
\ell_{1}
modes and
C_{2}
on
\ell_{2}
modes, then
\mathrm{D}
serialises the tensor by decoding the left block first and the right block afterwards.
Concretely,

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C)=\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s+\ell_{1}}_{a+1}(C_{2})\circ\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C_{1}).

By the induction hypothesis applied to
C_{1}
at offset
s
and to
C_{2}

at offset
s+\ell_{1}
,

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s+\ell_{1}}_{a}(C_{2})\bigr)\circ\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(C_{1})\bigr)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{s}_{a}(C)\bigr),

again by functoriality of
\mathtt{C}_{j}
.
∎

Lemma H.2
.

Let
C
be a circuit of
LOPP
acting on
\ell
consecutive modes, and fix

a\geq 0
and
s
with
s+\ell\leq d^{a}
. For every odd

j\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
we have

\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}j+s}_{a+1}(C)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{d^{a}-s-\ell}_{a}(\mathrm{Rev}(C))\bigr),

where
\mathrm{Rev}(C)
denotes the circuit obtained from
C
by
reversing the order of the
d^{a}
modes in the relevant block.

Proof.

This is the same induction as in Lemma
H.1
, but with one extra bookkeeping step reflecting the fact that odd Gray blocks are traversed in reverse order.
When
j
is odd, the block with leading digit
j
is read backwards by the Gray code:

G^{d}_{a+1}(d^{a}j+t)=j\cdot G^{d}_{a}(d^{a}-1-t).

For example, with
d=3
and
a=1
, the
j=1
block corresponds to the three consecutive mode indices
3,4,5
, but their Gray labels are
12,11,10
rather than
10,11,12
.
Thus, a subcircuit
C
acting on modes starting at offset
s
in that block corresponds to the same pattern as a forward-traversed circuit, but located at the mirrored starting position
d^{a}-s-\ell
.

The role of
\mathrm{Rev}(C)
is precisely to compensate for this reversal at the syntactic level: it renames mode
t
within the block to mode
(d^{a}-1-t)
.
Once
\mathrm{Rev}
is inserted, the decoding of each generator reduces to the even case with the mirrored offset, and then acquires the outer
\mathtt{C}_{j}
because the leading Gray digit is
j
throughout the block.

The inductive steps for sequential and parallel composition are identical to those in Lemma
H.1
, using the respective induction hypotheses and functoriality of
\mathtt{C}_{j}
.
∎

H.2.
Decoding GrayLifts

The operators
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{m}
and
j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{m}
are the optical gadgets used in the encoding to “lift” a local
d^{n}
-mode circuit through
m
additional Gray digits.
Intuitively,
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{m}(C)
is a block circuit consisting of
d^{m}
copies of
C
spread across the Gray blocks determined by the extra digits, mirroring the copy whenever the reflected Gray traversal reverses the local order.
Decoding should therefore produce
m
untouched qudits (identities), together with the same decoded action as
C
on the remaining
n
qudits.
Similarly,
j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{m}(C)
performs the same lift but only on the branch where a designated digit equals
j
; decoding should therefore add a
j
-control, again padded by
m
identities for the extra context digits.

We first record a simple arity property: each GrayLift layer multiplies the number of modes by
d
, corresponding to adding one more Gray digit.

Lemma H.3
.

Let
C
be a
LOPP
circuit acting on
d^{n}
modes. Then for every

k\geq 0
the circuit
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{k}(C)
acts on
d^{n+k}
modes.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
k
.
For
k=0
we have
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{0}(C)=C
by definition, so

\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{0}(C)
acts on
d^{n}
modes.

Assume the statement holds for some
k\geq 0
, i.e. that

\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{k}(C)
acts on
d^{n+k}
modes. By definition,

\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{k+1}(C)=\bigotimes_{q=0}^{d-1}\mathrm{Rev}^{q}\bigl(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{k}(C)\bigr).

The mode-reversal
\mathrm{Rev}^{q}(\,\cdot\,)
is implemented by a
permutation of modes and therefore does not change the number of
modes, so each tensor factor acts on
d^{n+k}
modes.
The tensor product of
d
such factors therefore acts on

d\cdot d^{n+k}=d^{n+k+1}
modes, completing the induction.
∎

Lemma H.4
.

Let
C
be a
LOPP
circuit acting on
d^{n}
modes, and let
m\geq 0
.
Then

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m}\bigl(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{m}(C)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{m}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)

as circuits in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

Proof.

We argue by induction on
m
.
It may help to first consider the case
m=1
:
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{1}(C)
is a tensor of
d
blocks, one per leading digit
j
, and decoding each block yields a
j
-controlled copy of
\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)
.
Composing these
d
controlled copies and using exhaustivity collapses the whole product to
\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)
.
The general case iterates this argument one Gray digit at a time.

Base case (
m=0
).
By definition
\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{0}(C)=C
, so

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}\bigl(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{0}(C)\bigr)=\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)=\mathrm{id}_{0}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C),

since
\mathrm{id}_{0}
is the monoidal unit.

Inductive step.
Assume the lemma holds for some
m\geq 0
, i.e.

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m}\bigl(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{m}(C)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{m}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C).

Set
F:=\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{m}(C)
, so by Lemma
H.3

F
acts on
d^{n+m}
modes.
By definition,

\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{m+1}(C)=\bigotimes_{j=0}^{d-1}\mathrm{Rev}^{j}(F),

a tensor of
d
blocks, each occupying a contiguous mode block of size
d^{n+m}
.

Decoding in an
(n+m+1)
-qudit context serialises the tensor product:

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m+1}\left(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{m+1}(C)\right)=\mathrm{D}^{d^{n+m}(d-1)}_{n+m+1}\bigl(\mathrm{Rev}^{d-1}(F)\bigr)\circ\cdots\circ\mathrm{D}^{d^{n+m}}_{n+m+1}\bigl(\mathrm{Rev}^{1}(F)\bigr)\circ\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m+1}\bigl(\mathrm{Rev}^{0}(F)\bigr).

For each
j
, the block
\mathrm{Rev}^{j}(F)
lives inside the Gray-code block with leading digit
j
.
Applying Lemma
H.1
when
j
is even and Lemma
H.2
when
j
is odd (always with
s=0
and
\ell=d^{n+m}
) yields, in either case,

\mathrm{D}^{d^{n+m}j}_{n+m+1}\bigl(\mathrm{Rev}^{j}(F)\bigr)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m}(F)\bigr).

Substituting back, we obtain

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m+1}\bigl(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{m+1}(C)\bigr)=\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m}(F)\bigr),

where the product is sequential composition in some fixed order.
By commutativity of distinct controls (Theorem
3.3
) the order does not matter, and by exhaustivity (Theorem
3.4
) the product collapses to

\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m}(F)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m}(F).

Using the induction hypothesis
\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m}(F)=\mathrm{id}_{m}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)
, we conclude

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+m+1}\bigl(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{m+1}(C)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes(\mathrm{id}_{m}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C))=\mathrm{id}_{m+1}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C).

∎

Lemma H.5
.

Let
C
be a
LOPP
circuit acting on
d^{n}
modes, and let
m\geq 0
.
Then

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+m}\bigl(j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{m}(C)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{m}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)\bigr)

as circuits in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
m
.
The base case is the branch-selection behaviour: only the block with leading digit
j
contains
C
, and decoding turns that into
\mathtt{C}_{j}(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C))
.
The inductive step then adds one more GrayLift layer, which by exhaustivity contributes exactly one more untouched qudit (an extra identity wire).

Base case (
m=0
).
By definition,
j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{0}(C)
is a tensor of
d
blocks on
d^{n}
modes each, where all blocks are identities except the
j
-th block, which contains
C
(mirrored if needed to match Gray traversal).
Decoding serialises this tensor into a product over
q\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
.
For
q\neq j
, the decoded factor is the identity, while for
q=j
we get, using Lemma
H.1
or Lemma
H.2
(depending on the parity of
j
),

\mathrm{D}^{d^{n}j}_{n+1}\bigl(\mathrm{Rev}^{j}(C)\bigr)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)\bigr).

Hence

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1}\bigl(j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{0}(C)\bigr)=\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{0}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)\bigr).

Inductive step.
Assume the lemma holds for some
m\geq 0
, i.e.

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+m}\bigl(j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{m}(C)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{m}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)\bigr).

Set
F:=j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{m}(C)
.
By the recursive definition of
j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}
, we have

j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{m+1}(C)=\bigotimes_{q=0}^{d-1}\mathrm{Rev}^{q}(F),

so decoding in an
(n+1+m+1)
-qudit context gives

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+m+1}\bigl(j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{m+1}(C)\bigr)=\prod_{q=0}^{d-1}\mathrm{D}^{d^{n+1+m}q}_{n+1+m+1}\bigl(\mathrm{Rev}^{q}(F)\bigr).

Applying Lemma
H.1
or Lemma
H.2
to each block (with
s=0
and
\ell=d^{n+1+m}
) yields

\mathrm{D}^{d^{n+1+m}q}_{n+1+m+1}\bigl(\mathrm{Rev}^{q}(F)\bigr)=\mathtt{C}_{q}\Bigl(\,\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+m}(F)\Bigr).

Therefore

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+m+1}\bigl(j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{m+1}(C)\bigr)=\prod_{q=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{q}\Bigl(\,\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+m}(F)\Bigr).

By exhaustivity (Theorem
3.4
),

\prod_{q=0}^{d-1}\mathtt{C}_{q}\Bigl(\,\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+m}(F)\Bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+m}(F).

Using the induction hypothesis for
F
,

\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n+1+m}(F)=\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes\bigl(\mathrm{id}_{m}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C))\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{m+1}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{n}(C)),

which is the desired statement for
m+1
.
∎

H.3.
Encoding and decoding are inverse

We can now prove that the contextual encoding and decoding are mutually inverse, up to padding with identities on the context wires.
The proof is a direct structural induction, but it is worth keeping the intended picture in mind:
the encoding of each primitive qudit constructor is built from (i) a small local optical circuit, (ii) GrayLift gadgets (
\mathrm{GrayLift}
or
j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}
) to place that circuit uniformly across Gray blocks, and (iii) block permutations
\sigma^{d}_{-,-,-}
to position the action on the correct qudit wires.
The decoding lemmas established above says that (ii) becomes either identity padding or a genuine control, and that (iii) becomes the corresponding qudit-wire symmetry.
After these wrappers are removed, what remains is exactly the original qudit generator.

Proof.

We prove Theorem
4.17
by structural induction on
f
.

Typing convention.
Fix
k,\ell\geq 0
and a circuit
f:n\to n
in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.
Then
E^{k}_{\ell}(f)
is a
d^{k+n+\ell}
-mode
LOPP
circuit, and the decoding appearing
in the theorem is
\mathrm{D}^{0}_{k+n+\ell}
.
For readability, throughout this proof we write

D(-)\ :=\ \mathrm{D}^{0}_{k+n+\ell}(-)

when the arity is clear from context.

Empty and identity.
If
f=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}
, then by definition

E^{k}_{\ell}(f)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}^{\otimes d^{k+\ell}},

and hence

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(f)\bigr)=D\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}^{\otimes d^{k+\ell}}\right)=\mathrm{id}_{k+\ell}=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{0}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}.

The case
f=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line0.pdf}}}}}}}$}
is similar.

Swap.
If
f=\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}
, the encoding uses the optical block-permutations

\sigma^{d}_{k,n,\ell}
:

E^{k}_{\ell}(\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}})=\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,2}\circ\sigma^{d}_{k+\ell,1,1}\circ\sigma^{d}_{k,2,\ell}.

Intuitively, these permutations implement the qudit-wire swap by reindexing Gray blocks of modes.
By Lemma
J.11
we have

D\bigl(\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,2}\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\sigma_{\ell,2},\quad D\bigl(\sigma^{d}_{k+\ell,1,1}\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k+\ell}\otimes\sigma_{1,1},\quad D\bigl(\sigma^{d}_{k,2,\ell}\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\sigma_{2,\ell}.

Therefore, using strict PROP coherence,

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}})\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\sigma_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}.

Global phase.
If
f=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}
then

E^{k}_{\ell}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})=\bigl(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\bigr)^{\otimes d^{k+\ell}},

so decoding produces one basis-controlled phase per Gray basis vector of the context register:

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\bigr)=\prod_{u\in[d]^{k+\ell}}\mathtt{C}_{u}\bigl(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\bigr).

For instance, when
k+\ell=1
this products is
\mathtt{C}_{0}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\circ\cdots\circ\mathtt{C}_{d-1}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})
, i.e. apply the same scalar phase on every basis value of the single context qudit.
By exhaustivity of control (Theorem
3.4
), the product over all words
u
yields

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k+\ell}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}.

Two-level Hadamard.
Let
f=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$}
. By Definition
4.8
we have

E^{k}_{\ell}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$})=\sigma^{d}_{k,1,\ell}\circ\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{k+\ell}\bigl(B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}\bigr)\circ\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,1}.

Applying
D
and functoriality for sequential composition gives

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$})\bigr)=D\bigl(\sigma^{d}_{k,1,\ell}\bigr)\circ D\Bigl(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{k+\ell}\bigl(B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}\bigr)\Bigr)\circ D\bigl(\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,1}\bigr).

By Lemma
J.11
,

D\bigl(\sigma^{d}_{k,1,\ell}\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\sigma_{1,\ell},\qquad D\bigl(\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,1}\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\sigma_{\ell,1}.

By Lemma
H.4
, instantiated with
C=B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}
and
m=k+\ell
,

D\Bigl(\mathrm{GrayLift}_{d}^{k+\ell}\bigl(B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}\bigr)\Bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k+\ell}\otimes\mathrm{D}^{0}_{1}\bigl(B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}\bigr).

Finally, by the choice of
B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}
(Lemma
G.1
) and the decoding correctness for this fixed single-qudit network,

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{1}\bigl(B_{d}^{(i,i+1)}\bigr)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$}.

Combining these equalities and simplifying using PROP coherence yields

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$})\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Hadiip.pdf}}}}}}}$}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}.

Sequential composition.
If
f=g\circ h
, then by Definition
4.8
,

E^{k}_{\ell}(g\circ h)=E^{k}_{\ell}(g)\circ E^{k}_{\ell}(h),

so

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(g\circ h)\bigr)=D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(g)\bigr)\circ D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(h)\bigr).

The induction hypothesis gives

D(E^{k}_{\ell}(g))=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes g\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}
and

D(E^{k}_{\ell}(h))=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}
, hence

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(g\circ h)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes(g\circ h)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}.

Parallel composition.
If
f=g\otimes h
with
g:n_{g}\to n_{g}
and
h:n_{h}\to n_{h}
, then
Definition
4.8
serialises the tensor product:

E^{k}_{\ell}(g\otimes h)=E^{k+n_{g}}_{\ell}(h)\circ E^{k}_{\ell+n_{h}}(g).

Decoding preserves
\circ
, so

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(g\otimes h)\bigr)=D\bigl(E^{k+n_{g}}_{\ell}(h)\bigr)\circ D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell+n_{h}}(g)\bigr).

Applying the induction hypotheses to
h
at context
(k+n_{g},\ell)
and to
g
at
context
(k,\ell+n_{h})
yields

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(g\otimes h)\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes(g\otimes h)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}.

Control.
Finally, let
f=\mathtt{C}_{j}(g)
with
g:m\to m
.
By Definition
4.8
,

E^{k}_{\ell}\bigl(\mathtt{C}_{j}(g)\bigr)=\sigma^{d}_{k,m+1,\ell}\circ j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{k+\ell}\bigl(E^{0}_{0}(g)\bigr)\circ\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,m+1}.

Decoding and using Lemma
J.11
reduces the outer permutations to the corresponding
qudit-wire symmetries.
For the middle term we use Lemma
H.5
to obtain

D\Bigl(j\mathrm{Gray}\mathrm{Lift}_{d}^{k+\ell}\bigl(E^{0}_{0}(g)\bigr)\Bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k+\ell}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}\bigl(\mathrm{D}^{0}_{m}(E^{0}_{0}(g))\bigr).

By the induction hypothesis applied to
g
with
k=\ell=0
,

\mathrm{D}^{0}_{m}(E^{0}_{0}(g))=g
, hence the middle factor is
\mathrm{id}_{k+\ell}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}(g)
,
and the surrounding symmetries cancel by PROP coherence.
Therefore

D\bigl(E^{k}_{\ell}(\mathtt{C}_{j}(g))\bigr)=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{C}_{j}(g)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}.

This exhausts the constructors of
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
, hence

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash\mathrm{D}^{0}_{k+n+\ell}(E^{k}_{\ell}(f))=\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\ell}

for all
f
, as required.
∎

Appendix I
Mimicking rules

This appendix proves Theorem
4.16
from
Section
4
: every equational step in the LOPP calculus can
be
mimicked
inside our qudit theory via decoding.
Concretely, for any
n\geq 0
and any
d^{n}
-mode LOPP circuits
C_{1},C_{2}
, if

\mathrm{LOPP}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}
then their decodings satisfy

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash D(C_{1})=D(C_{2})
.

The proof has two independent checks:

(1)

decoding is compatible with the strict-PROP congruence on raw LOPP terms
(i.e. the strict symmetric monoidal/PROP equations);

(2)

decoding maps each non-structural axiom of the LOPP calculus
(Figure
9
) to a derivable equation in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
.

Since both equational theories are congruences closed under arbitrary contexts,
these two checks suffice to mimic arbitrary LOPP derivations.

We work with
raw
circuits, i.e. string diagrams freely generated from the
LOPP (or qudit) generators using
\circ
and
\otimes
, before quotienting by any
equations.
We use
\equiv
for the strict-PROP congruence on raw LOPP circuits as in
Definition
4.1
. For convenience we reproduce the generating
equations in Figure
8
.

(24)

\begin{array}[t]{rcccl}id_{k}\circ C&\equiv&C&\equiv&C\circ id_{k}\\[5.0pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Cidmultifils.pdf}}}}}}}$}&\equiv&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/Cmultifils.pdf}}}}}}}$}&\equiv&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/idCmultifils.pdf}}}}}}}$}\end{array}

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}(C_{3}\circ C_{2})\circ C_{1}&\equiv&C_{3}\circ(C_{2}\circ C_{1})\\[5.0pt]

(26)

\begin{array}[t]{rcccl}\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes C&\equiv&C&\equiv&C\otimes\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\\[5.0pt]

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}(C_{2}\circ C_{1})\otimes(C_{4}\circ C_{3})&\equiv&(C_{2}\otimes C_{4})\circ(C_{1}\otimes C_{3})\\[5.0pt]

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\circ\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}&\equiv&\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\\[5.0pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swapswap.pdf}}}}}}}$}&\equiv&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/two-lines.pdf}}}}}}}$}\end{array}

where
id_{0}=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/empty_diagram.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}
and
id_{k+1}=id_{k}\otimes\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}
, and

\sigma_{0}:=\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}
,

\sigma_{k+1}:=(\scalebox{.5}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes id_{k})\circ(\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\otimes\sigma_{k})
.

Figure 8
.
Structural axioms generating the congruence
\equiv
on raw
circuits (either raw quantum circuits or raw optical circuits).
Structural (strict symmetric monoidal/PROP) equations on raw circuits:
unit and associativity for composition and tensor; interchange; naturality of swap; and involutivity of swap.
Each equation is shown both algebraically and as a string diagram.

Commutation of decoded circuits on disjoint blocks

We first record a basic commuting property of decoding when two optical
subcircuits act on disjoint mode intervals.

Lemma I.1
.

Let
C_{1}:\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}
and
C_{2}:\ell_{2}\to\ell_{2}
be raw optical
circuits. Let
n\geq 0
and let
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy

0\leq t_{1}
,
0\leq t_{2}
,
t_{1}+\ell_{1}\leq t_{2}
, and
t_{2}+\ell_{2}\leq d^{n}
.
Then

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\ \vdash\ \mathrm{D}^{t_{2}}_{n}(C_{2})\ \circ\ \mathrm{D}^{t_{1}}_{n}(C_{1})=\mathrm{D}^{t_{1}}_{n}(C_{1})\ \circ\ \mathrm{D}^{t_{2}}_{n}(C_{2}).

Proof.

We argue by structural induction on the pair
(C_{1},C_{2})
.

If either circuit is a sequential composite, the statement follows immediately
from the induction hypothesis and the defining clause

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{2}\circ C_{1})=\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{2})\circ\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{1})
.

If either circuit is a tensor composite, we use the serialisation clause

\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{1}\otimes C_{2})=\mathrm{D}^{t+\ell_{1}}_{n}(C_{2})\circ\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(C_{1})

(Definition
4.11
) together with associativity of
\circ
to reduce
to commuting decoded subcircuits whose acted-on mode intervals remain disjoint;
the required commutations are then provided by the induction hypothesis.

Thus it suffices to treat the case where both
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are LOPP
generators (phase shifter, beam splitter, or swap).
By Lemma
4.12
, each decoded generator acts non-trivially
on at most one basis vector (phase shifter) or one two-dimensional Gray-neighbour
subspace (beam splitter/swap). Since the mode intervals are disjoint, these
supports are disjoint in the Gray-ordered computational basis, and the
corresponding decoded operations commute in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
using the derived
distinct-control commutativity principle together with the local commutation
schemata of Figure
2
.
∎

Contexts and compatibility with structural congruence

To phrase compatibility with
\equiv
it is convenient to work with contexts.

Definition I.2.

A (one-hole) raw context
\mathbb{C}[\cdot]_{i}
is a term over the LOPP signature built
from
\circ
and
\otimes
with a distinguished placeholder of arity
i
.
If the outer arity of
\mathbb{C}[\cdot]_{i}
is
N
, we say that
\mathbb{C}[\cdot]_{i}
is an

N
-mode context with hole arity
i
.

Definition I.3.

Given a
N
-mode context
\mathbb{C}[\cdot]_{i}
and an
i
-mode raw circuit
C
, we write

\mathbb{C}[C]
for the
N
-mode circuit obtained by filling the hole in
\mathbb{C}[\cdot]_{i}

with
C
.

Lemma I.4
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}
be
d^{n}
-mode raw optical circuits. If
C_{1}\equiv C_{2}
, then

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\ \vdash\ D(C_{1})=D(C_{2}).

Proof.

We prove the following offset-and-context strengthening: for any context

\mathbb{C}[\cdot]_{i}
, any
i
-mode circuits
C_{1},C_{2}
, and any
t,n
with

t+N\leq d^{n}
(where
N
is the outer arity of
\mathbb{C}[\cdot]_{i}
),

C_{1}\equiv C_{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{QC}_{d}\ \vdash\ \mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(\mathbb{C}[C_{1}])=\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}(\mathbb{C}[C_{2}]).

The desired statement is the special case
\mathbb{C}[\cdot]_{i}=[\cdot]_{i}
and
t=0
.

By induction on the context structure it suffices to check preservation of the
generating axioms of Figure
8
. Identities and tensor units are
sent to
\mathrm{id}_{n}
by Definition
4.11
. Associativity of
\circ
is
preserved because
\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}
is defined compositionally on
\circ
.
Associativity of
\otimes
is preserved because
\mathrm{D}^{t}_{n}
serialises
\otimes

into a fixed sequential composite, and the two bracketings decode to the same
composite up to associativity of
\circ
.
For the mixed-product/interchange axiom (
29
), expanding both sides
via the serialisation clause yields composites of decoded subcircuits acting on
disjoint mode blocks; Lemma
I.1
provides the required
commutations.
Naturality and involutivity of swap follow from the fact that decoding of
permutation circuits agrees with the corresponding qudit-wire symmetries (see
Appendix
J
).
∎

Mimicking the LOPP equational theory

Recall the non-structural axioms of LOPP (Figure
9
).

(A)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-two-phase.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase-add.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(
2\pi
)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/lov-phase-2pi.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/line.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(SW)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap-equiv.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(E)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/3beamsplitter-left.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/3beamsplitter-right.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(3BS)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/3xrot-left.pdf}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/3xrot-right.pdf}}}}}}}$}

Figure 9
.
Non-structural axioms of the LOPP calculus
(
18
)
:
phase addition,
2\pi
-periodicity, swap decomposition, and
Euler/three-beamsplitter identities.
Five non-structural LOPP rewrite axioms: phase addition on one mode;
$2\pi$-periodicity; a swap decomposition into phases and beam splitters; and two
standard identities involving three beam splitters (Euler form and a 3-beam-splitter relation).

Theorem I.5
.

For any
d^{n}
-mode raw optical circuits
C_{1},C_{2}
, if

\mathrm{LOPP}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}
, then

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\ \vdash\ D(C_{1})=D(C_{2}).

Proof.

The LOPP derivability relation is the congruence generated by the strict-PROP
axioms (Figure
8
) and the non-structural axioms
(
A
)–(
3BS
).
By Lemma
I.4
, decoding respects the strict-PROP congruence,
so it suffices to verify that each non-structural axiom is preserved by decoding
at an arbitrary offset
t
(closure under contexts is handled exactly as in the
proof of Lemma
I.4
).

Let
t,n
be such that the relevant local LOPP circuit sits inside
d^{n}
modes.

•

Phase addition
(
A
).
Decoding a phase shifter on mode
t
yields

\mathtt{C}_{G_{n}^{d}(t)}(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/phase.pdf}}}}}}}$})
(Definition
4.11
).
The decoded equation is therefore an instance of the
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
phase
arithmetic axiom (
Sum
), closed under iterated control.

•

2\pi
-periodicity
(

⁢
2
π

).
Similarly, decoding

yields a multi-controlled

2\pi
-phase, which is equal to
\mathrm{id}_{n}
by the
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
axiom (

⁢
2
π

)
(again closed under iterated control). The right-hand side decodes to
\mathrm{id}_{n}
.

•

Swap decomposition
(
SW
).
This is a two-mode local identity. Decoding at offset
t
yields a
(multi-)controlled two-level swap on the unique Gray-neighbour subspace
determined by modes
t,t+1
(Definition
4.11
).
The right-hand side decodes to the same controlled two-level swap by a
direct diagrammatic calculation in
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
, using the axioms
(
TP
) and (
TH
) together with the
core two-level gate identities of Figure
1
.

•

Euler identity
(
E
).
Decoding sends each beam splitter on the consecutive modes
t,t+1
to a
(multi-)controlled two-level rotation

on the corresponding
Gray-neighbour computational subspace. The resulting decoded equation is an
instance of the
\mathrm{QC}_{d}
Euler schema (
EH
) (closed under
control and conjugation by the surrounding wire permutations used in

\Lambda^{u}_{w}(-)
).

•

Three-beamsplitter identity
(
3BS
).
Decoding of the three-mode local pattern depends only on which Gray digit
changes between
t,t+1,t+2
(Lemma
4.5
).
If all three consecutive modes lie in a single adjacent-level chain for one
digit, the decoded equation is an instance of (
3Rx
).
Otherwise, one of the rotation is additionally controlled by a fixed value
of another digit, and the decoded equation is an instance of (
3CRx
).

Thus decoding preserves each generator of the LOPP equational theory and is
stable under contexts; hence
\mathrm{LOPP}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}
implies

\mathrm{QC}_{d}\vdash D(C_{1})=D(C_{2})
.
∎

Appendix J
Explicit encoding and decoding of swaps

In this section we spell out the encoding and decoding of wire-swaps in the
LOPP calculus, and show that the optical block-swaps used in the encoding
indeed decode to the intended qudit-level swaps in
\textbf{{CQC}}_{d}
.

J.1.
Gray-code indices for words

We first fix a convenient notation for adressing modes in Gray-code order.
Recall that a mode in the
d
-ary Gray code for
n
qudits is labelled by a
word
w\in[d]^{n}
. We now define the inverse map, which returns the position
of a given word in the Gray ordering.

Definition J.1.

For a base-
d
word
w\in[d]^{n}
we write
G^{-1}_{d}(w)
for its index
in the
d
-ary Gray ordering. It is defined recursively as follows.

•

For the empty word
\epsilon
we set
G^{-1}_{d}(\epsilon)=0
.

•

For a non-empty word
w=h\cdot t
of length
n+1
, with

h\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
the first digit and
t\in[d]^{n}
the suffix,
we put

G^{-1}_{d}(h\cdot t)=\begin{cases}h\,d^{n}+G^{-1}_{d}(t)&\text{if $h$ is even},\\[1.72218pt]
(h+1)\,d^{n}-1-G^{-1}_{d}(t)&\text{if $h$ is odd}.\end{cases}

Intuitively, when the leading digit
h
is even, the block

\{h\}\times[d]^{n}
appears in the Gray ordering in increasing order of
t
;
when
h
is odd, the block is traversed in reverse order. This mirrors the
usual “reflect-and-prefix” construction of Gray codes.

J.2.
Elementary LOPP swap gadgets

We now define an optical gadget that swaps two basis states.

Definition J.2.

Let
w_{1},w_{2}\in[d]^{n}
be two distinct words of the same length. We write

C_{d,w_{1},w_{2}}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/C-swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}

for the LOPP circuit that swaps the two modes labelled by
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
and
acts as the identity on all other modes.

In what follows we use this notation also in more structured instances such as

C_{d,\beta\cdot q_{1}\cdot\alpha,\ \beta\cdot p_{1}\cdot\alpha}
, where

\beta
and
\alpha
denote fixed prefixes/suffixes and the middle digit
changes from
q_{1}
to
p_{1}
.

At the level of decoding, the basic behaviour is as expected:

- Decoding
C_{d,\beta\cdot q_{1}\cdot\alpha,\ \beta\cdot p_{1}\cdot\alpha}

yields a two-level
X^{(q_{1},p_{1})}
on the corresponding qudit, with all
other qudits providing (multi-)controls determined by
\beta
and
\alpha
.

- Decoding
C_{d,\beta\cdot q_{1}\cdot q_{2}\cdot\alpha,\,\beta\cdot p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\cdot\alpha}

yields a pattern of three such controlled
X
gates, i.e. three
CNOT-like gates acting on the appropriate two-level subspace.

To remove the additional controls arising from
\beta
and
\alpha
, we
introduce a “saturation” of these gadgets over all possible prefix/suffix
values.

Definition J.3.

For integers
k,\ell\geq 0
and digits
q_{1},q_{2},p_{1},p_{2}\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}

we define

C^{\star}_{d,k,\ell,[q_{1},q_{2}],[p_{1},p_{2}]}=\prod_{\beta=0}^{d^{k-1}-1}\ \prod_{\alpha=0}^{d^{\ell}-1}C_{d,\ \beta\cdot q_{1}\cdot q_{2}\cdot\alpha,\ \beta\cdot p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\cdot\alpha}.

Intuitively,
C^{\star}
applies the same three-CNOT pattern for every choice
of prefix
\beta
and suffix
\alpha
. By exhaustivity and commuting of
controls (Thms.
3.3
and
3.4
), these extra
controls cancel when decoding:

Lemma J.4
.

We have

D\left(C^{\star}_{d,k,\ell,[q_{1},q_{2}],[p_{1},p_{2}]}\right)=id_{k}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/threeCNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}\otimes id_{\ell}.

Proof.

By definition,

\displaystyle D\bigl(C^{\star}_{d,k,\ell,[q_{1},q_{2}],[p_{1},p_{2}]}\bigr)

\displaystyle=D\left(\prod_{\beta,\alpha}C_{d,\beta\cdot q_{1}\cdot q_{2}\cdot\alpha,\,\beta\cdot p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\cdot\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\beta,\alpha}D\left(C_{d,\beta\cdot q_{1}\cdot q_{2}\cdot\alpha,\,\beta\cdot p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\cdot\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\beta,\alpha}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/threeCNOTab.pdf}}}}}}}$}.

Each factor

acts on the same pair of target levels and
differs only by its outer controls; by commutation and exhaustivity of
controls (Thms.
3.3
and
3.4
) the product
simplifies to a single three-CNOT pattern with no remaining controls, tensored
with identities on the untouched wires. This yields the claimed form.
∎

J.3.
Recursive construction of word-swaps

To describe the optical implementation
C_{d,w_{1},w_{2}}
more concretely, it is
convenient to use a parity based notation on words.

Definition J.5.

For a word
w=h_{1}\cdots h_{m}\in[d]^{m}
define its (binary) parity by

\texttt{Parity}(w)=(h_{1}\bmod 2)\oplus\cdots\oplus(h_{m}\bmod 2)\in\{0,1\},

where
\oplus
is addition modulo 2.

Given a word
u
and an integer
n\geq 0
, the
parity tail

\texttt{ParityTail}_{u,n}\in[d]^{n}
is

\texttt{ParityTail}_{u,n}=\begin{cases}0^{n}&\text{if }\texttt{Parity}(u)=0,\\
(d{-}1)^{n}&\text{if }\texttt{Parity}(u)=1.\end{cases}

The following inductive clauses specify
C_{d,w_{1},w_{2}}
for arbitrary

w_{1},w_{2}\in[d]^{m}
.

(1)

Base case (adjacent Gray neighbours).

Suppose

w_{1}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot p\cdot\mathsf{suf},\qquad w_{2}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot q\cdot\mathsf{suf},

with
|q-p|=1
and

\mathsf{suf}=\texttt{ParityTail}_{\mathsf{pref}\cdot\max(p,q),\,|\mathsf{suf}|}
.
Then
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are consecutive in Gray order, and we set

C_{d,w_{1},w_{2}}=id_{G^{-1}_{d}(\mathsf{pref}\cdot\min(p,q)\cdot\mathsf{suf})}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/swap.pdf}}}}}}}$}\otimes id_{d^{|w_{1}|}-G^{-1}_{d}(\mathsf{pref}\cdot\max(p,q)\cdot\mathsf{suf})-1}.

(2)

Adjusting the suffix.

Suppose again
w_{1}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot p\cdot\mathsf{suf}
and

w_{2}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot q\cdot\mathsf{suf}
with
|q-p|=1
, but

\mathsf{suf}
is not already a parity tail. Write

\mathsf{suf}=\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{2}
such that

\alpha_{2}=\texttt{ParityTail}_{\mathsf{pref}\cdot\max(p,q)\cdot\alpha_{1},\,|\alpha_{2}|}
and

\alpha_{i}\neq\texttt{ParityTail}_{\mathsf{pref}\cdot\max(p,q)\cdot\alpha_{1},1}
.
Let

\alpha_{i}^{\prime}=\alpha_{i}+(-1)^{\texttt{Parity}(\mathsf{pref}\cdot\alpha_{1}\cdot\min(p,q))}

and define

w_{1}^{\prime}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot p\cdot\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\alpha_{2},\qquad w_{2}^{\prime}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot q\cdot\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\alpha_{2}.

Then we set

C_{d,w_{1},w_{2}}=C_{d,w_{1},w_{1}^{\prime}}\circ C_{d,w_{2},w_{2}^{\prime}}\circ C_{d,w_{1}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime}}\circ C_{d,w_{2},w_{2}^{\prime}}\circ C_{d,w_{1},w_{1}^{\prime}}.

(3)

Bridging distant levels.

Suppose
w_{1}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot p\cdot\mathsf{suf}
and

w_{2}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot q\cdot\mathsf{suf}
with
|q-p|>1
.
Let

w_{\max}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot\max(p,q)\cdot\mathsf{suf},\quad w_{\max-1}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot\bigl(\max(p,q)-1\bigr)\cdot\mathsf{suf},\quad w_{\min}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot\min(p,q)\cdot\mathsf{suf}.

We then define

C_{d,w_{1},w_{2}}=C_{d,w_{\max},w_{\max-1}}\circ C_{d,w_{\min},w_{\max-1}}\circ C_{d,w_{\max},w_{\max-1}}.

(4)

Words differing in two positions.

Suppose

w_{1}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot p_{1}\cdot\mathsf{mid}\cdot p_{2}\cdot\mathsf{suf},\qquad w_{2}=\mathsf{pref}^{\prime}\cdot q_{1}\cdot\mathsf{mid}\cdot q_{2}\cdot\mathsf{suf}

with
p_{1}\neq q_{1}
and
p_{2}\neq q_{2}
. Let

w_{2}^{\prime}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot q_{1}\cdot\mathsf{mid}\cdot q_{2}\cdot\mathsf{suf},\qquad w^{\prime}=\mathsf{pref}\cdot p_{1}\cdot\mathsf{mid}\cdot q_{2}\cdot\mathsf{suf}.

Then we set

C_{d,w_{1},w_{2}}=C_{d,w_{2},w_{2}^{\prime}}\circ C_{d,w_{1},w^{\prime}}\circ C_{d,w^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime}}\circ C_{d,w_{1},w^{\prime}}\circ C_{d,w_{2},w_{2}^{\prime}}.

The four clauses above recursively express an arbitrary word-swap as a
composition of adjacent Gray-neighbour swaps, possibly after “straightening”
suffixes and separating differences across positions.

J.4.
Decoding of the recursive swap construction

We now record the effect of decoding these gadgets.

Lemma J.6
.

For any digits
p_{1},q_{1}
and words
\alpha,\beta
we have

D\bigl(C_{d,\ \beta\cdot q_{1}\cdot\alpha,\ \beta\cdot p_{1}\cdot\alpha}\bigr)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/oneCNOTab.pdf}}}}}}}$},

i.e. a single two-level
X^{(p_{1},q_{1})}
on the appropriate qudit, plus
trivial identities on the other wires.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on the definition of
C_{d,w_{1},w_{2}}
.

In the base case
1
the two words are adjacent in Gray order,
and
C_{d,w_{1},w_{2}}
is literally a single wire-swap between the corresponding
modes, so the decoding is immediate.

In case
3
, the definition expresses the swap between
levels
p_{1}
and
q_{1}
as a composite of swaps involving the intermediate
level
M_{1}=\max(p_{1},q_{1})
and
M_{1}{-}1
. Using
Lemma
XX2
together with involutivity (
XInv
) one obtains
the required single
X^{(p_{1},q_{1})}
on decoding:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/pr-sw-2-0.pdf}}}}}}}$}\overset{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{Xij-Xjk}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/pr-sw-2-1.pdf}}}}}}}$}\overset{\eqref{Xij-Xij}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/oneCNOTab.pdf}}}}}}}$}.

In case
2
, the suffix-adjustment circuit is a
five-fold composition of swaps. Using repeatedly the three-CX commutation
pattern (
CX–XC–CX
) and symmetry of two-level
X
gates
(By definition), followed again by (
XInv
), one reduces this
pattern to a single
X^{(p_{1},q_{1})}
:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/pr-swap-c0.pdf}}}}}}}$}\overset{\eqref{CX-XC-CX}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/pr-swap-c1.pdf}}}}}}}$}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{CX-XC-CX}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/pr-swap-c2.pdf}}}}}}}$}\overset{\eqref{Xij-Xij}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/pr-swap-c3.pdf}}}}}}}$}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{Xij-Xij}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/pr-swap-c4.pdf}}}}}}}$}.

The remaining case
4
will be handled in the next
lemma. This completes the proof for the present statement.
∎

Lemma J.7
.

For any digits
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}
and words
\alpha,\beta
we have

D\bigl(C_{d,\ \beta\cdot q_{1}\cdot q_{2}\cdot\alpha,\ \beta\cdot p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\cdot\alpha}\bigr)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/threeCNOTab.pdf}}}}}}}$}.

Proof.

In case
4
the two words differ in two positions, and
the recursive definition factors the swap into a composition of three
single-position swaps, to which Lemma
J.6
applies. The result
is exactly the depicted three-CNOT pattern.
∎

Combining these with Lemma
J.4
we obtain:

Lemma J.8
.

For all
k,\ell
and digits
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}
we have

D\bigl(C^{\star}_{d,k,\ell,[q_{1},q_{2}],[p_{1},p_{2}]}\bigr)=id_{k}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/threeCNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}\otimes id_{\ell}.

J.5.
Decoding block-swaps

We now assemble block-swaps from these saturated gadgets.

Definition J.9.

For integers
k,\ell\geq 0
we define the LOPP block-swap

\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,1}
that exchanges a block of
\ell
modes with a single
mode to its right by

\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,1}=\prod_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\prod_{x=0}^{d-2}\prod_{y=x+1}^{d-1}C^{\star}_{d,k+j,\ell-j-1,\,[x,y],[y,x]}.

Lemma J.10
.

We have

D\left(\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,1}\right)=id_{k}\otimes\sigma_{\ell,1},

where
\sigma_{\ell,1}
is the usual PROP symmetry swapping an
\ell
-wire
block with a single wire.

Proof.

Using Lemma
J.8
we obtain

\displaystyle D\left(\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,1}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\ \prod_{x=0}^{d-2}\ \prod_{y=x+1}^{d-1}D\left(C^{\star}_{d,k+j,\ell-j-1,[x,y],[y,x]}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\ \prod_{x=0}^{d-2}\ \prod_{y=x+1}^{d-1}\left(id_{k+j}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/threeCNOTi.pdf}}}}}}}$}\otimes id_{\ell-j-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\Bigl(id_{k+j}\otimes\underbrace{\Bigl(\prod_{x<y}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/threeCNOTi.pdf}}}}}}}$}\Bigr)}_{\overset{\eqref{swap-decomp}}{=}\sigma_{1,1}}\otimes id_{\ell-j-1}\Bigr).

The product over
x<y
yields exactly the adjacent swap
\sigma_{1,1}
by the
swap decomposition rule (
S
). Factoring out the leftmost
k

wires, we obtain

\displaystyle D\left(\sigma^{d}_{k,\ell,1}\right)

\displaystyle=id_{k}\otimes\Bigl(\prod_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\bigl(id_{j}\otimes\sigma_{1,1}\otimes id_{\ell-j-1}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=id_{k}\otimes\sigma_{\ell,1},

since the product on the right is precisely the standard factorisation of a
block-swap into
\ell
adjacent transpositions.
∎

Finally, we generalise from swapping a single mode with an
\ell
-block to
swapping two arbitrary blocks.

Lemma J.11
.

Define inductively

\sigma^{d}_{a,b,c}:=\sigma^{d}_{a,b+c-1,1}\circ\sigma^{d}_{a,b+1,c-1}.

Then

D\left(\sigma^{d}_{a,b,c}\right)=id_{a}\otimes\sigma_{b,c}.

Proof.

By definition,

\displaystyle D\left(\sigma^{d}_{a,b,c}\right)

\displaystyle=D\left(\sigma^{d}_{a,b+c-1,1}\circ\sigma^{d}_{a,b+1,c-1}\right)

\displaystyle=D\left(\sigma^{d}_{a,b+c-1,1}\right)\circ D\left(\sigma^{d}_{a,b+1,c-1}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:dec-swap-1}}{=}\bigl(id_{a}\otimes\sigma_{b+c-1,1}\bigr)\circ D\left(\sigma^{d}_{a,b+1,c-1}\right).

By the induction hypothesis on
c
we have

D(\sigma^{d}_{a,b+1,c-1})=id_{a}\otimes\sigma_{b+1,c-1}
, so

\displaystyle D\left(\sigma^{d}_{a,b,c}\right)

\displaystyle=id_{a}\otimes\bigl(\sigma_{b+c-1,1}\circ\sigma_{b+1,c-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=id_{a}\otimes\sigma_{b,c},

since
\sigma_{b,c}
is given by first moving the rightmost wire across the
block of size
b+c{-}1
and then swapping the resulting
(b+1)
-block with
the remaining
c{-}1
wires. This is exactly the composition

\sigma_{b+c-1,1}\circ\sigma_{b+1,c-1}
.
∎
